## Day 1044

*Date: 2025-01-02 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*Qui Ose Gagne*

[Image]

Lovely sunset on the first day of the year.

[Image]

Yesterday morning a drone creamed a residential flat a stone’s throw away from the President’s Office in downtown Kyiv, according to [The New York Times](http://nyt.com).

> […] Wreckage from one drone fell on a residential building only a few minutes from the Presidential Office, partly destroying the residential building’s top two floors and causing a fire, according to Ukrainian emergency services. Two people were killed and seven others were wounded across the city, local officials said[1](#footnote-1).

Hours earlier, in his New Year’s speech, Z faced northeast, looking in the same direction as the Rodina Mat’ statue, with his stern face staring straight ahead, symbolizing vigilance and defense[2](#footnote-2).

The American flag was superimposed on Rodina Mat’ during bits of his overly-produced 20-minute pep talk.

---

The president has been telling us for months that before new military brigades can be created and deployed Ukraine’s partners must provide pledged equipment and training.

France was supposed to outfit and train up the [Anna Kyivska 155th Mechanized Brigade](https://mil.in.ua/en/news/france-completes-training-of-anna-of-kyiv-brigade/), but something went terribly wrong.

> In December, the State Bureau of Investigation opened a criminal case into the circumstances of the formation of the brigade, which entered the battle near Pokrovsk. The case is under the control of the Supreme Commander-in-Chief Z, Defense Minister Umerov and Commander-in-Chief of the Armed Forces of Ukraine Oleksandr Syrsky. One of the leaders responsible for the formation of the brigade died of a heart attack, and the brigade commander was dismissed immediately after the brigade entered the battle. Before the brigade fired the first shot, 1,700 servicemen left it voluntarily [went AWOL]. SBI investigators were tasked with finding the culprits, and I conducted an investigation, obtained a significant amount of testimony and materials to show the public what the formation of new brigades […] from scratch, without the presence of command personnel, technical specialists, weapons, and drones leads to. **This is indeed a crime, but it is not a crime of soldiers and officers – but a crime of the leaders of the Supreme Commander-in-Chief's Headquarters, the Ministry of Defense, and the General Staff, who continue to waste lives and state funds on new projects, instead of strengthening experienced and combat-ready brigades. And they are trying to shirk responsibility by shifting it from the hands of the SBI to military officers in a hopeless situation who have to answer for a political project poorly organized by the top leadership**. — Yuriy Butusov ([Facebook](https://www.facebook.com/butusov.yuriy/posts/pfbid02r975da4kSEnK5PE5eVmn2KEYAQhf6QynE2eHSHNQZ6GZKJK6iLNgxq6xuCYbDEk2l))

Official PR was up to snuff, kind of, until it wasn’t. [The mess is now in the western press](https://www.forbes.com/sites/davidaxe/2025/01/01/ukraines-newest-leopard-2-brigade-began-disintegrating-before-it-reached-the-front-line/). We warned you about [noticeably renewed vigor](https://abdymok.substack.com/p/day-752?utm_source=publication-search) on Day 752.

[Image]

Above is a map of Russia’s missile attack on December 31, 2024.

## Day 1045 

*Date: 2025-01-03 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*Flawed and impractical *

[Image]

A new cover for a book that does not as yet exist.

Matthew Livelsberger, a highly decorated active duty US Army Green Beret, shot himself in the head seconds before self-immolation. The 37-year old soldier was home on leave from Stuttgart, Germany.

The New Year’s Day explosion outside Trump International Hotel in Las Vegas was reported about 8:40 a.m. local time, [police said](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dGOmJUMTWWA).

> "We were told that a 2024 Cybertruck pulled up to the front of the hotel, and, in fact, I can tell you it pulled right up to the glass entrance doors of the hotel. We saw smoke start showing from the vehicle, and then an explosion from the truck occurs."

The screen grab reminded me instantly of the picture snapped just after the assassination eight years ago of Russia’s ambassador to Turkey at an art exhibit in Ankara, Turkey.

[Image]

The gunman, Mevlut Mert Altıntas, an officer in Ankara’s riot police squad, yelled “Don’t forget Aleppo. Don’t forget Syria” before he was shot dead.

[Image]

A third jacket cover can be lifted from Z’s annual holiday speech featuring a not too subtle visual hint of a waving American flag superimposed on the titanium Mat’ Rodina monument.

Not unlike photographs, human speech is also composed of figurative expressions. The photographic representation of reality is a metaphorical image of another reality, which remains visually absent. Similarly, words which may be obscured or totally lacking in a tirade can be *understood* by the context of the presentation. It is not so much that the speech sounds "contain" meaning; it is rather that they "trigger" meaning.

## Day 1049

*Date: 2025-01-07 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*Four systemic failures*

[Keith has postponed his visit.](https://www.reuters.com/world/us/trumps-ukraine-envoy-postpones-kyiv-trip-until-after-inauguration-2025-01-06/) He obvi got the memo.

1. Fortifications (not built, built too late, built very poorly in wrong places)[1](#footnote-1)
2. Conscription (systemic failure)[2](#footnote-2)
3. New brigades (systemic failure)[3](#footnote-3)
4. Domestic arms/ammo production (systemic failure)[4](#footnote-4)

The Peace Formula, Victory and Resiliency Plans are, er, forgotten.

Relying on the angels of Donald’s aspirationally masculine bro squad (Lex, Pete, Joe and Elon) isn’t a great idea, and Z’s continuing detachment from reality remains an impediment.

[Image]

No one in Ukraine should count on Donald to do the right thing. Ever.

[Maksym Zhorin](https://t.me/MaksymZhorin/5534), nicknamed “Mose,” at war as a volunteer since 2014 and today deputy commander of the 3rd Brigade, sums up the current predicament as follows.

> I don't really believe that any situation can mobilize society. Our enemy is already a few kilometers away in the Dnipropetrovsk region, and nobody gives a f\*\*\*. It is unlikely that anything external [to Ukraine] will be able to change people’s mindset.
>
> Only domestic policy can mobilize: the approach, attitude to people, proper information work, and, above all, justice in the country.
>
> Today, there are big problems starting with mobilization and ending with corruption in absolutely all areas. Without justice, a society where everyone works for the same goal is impossible. And without this, in turn, the struggle is impossible.

Z, meanwhile, sounds like a damaged vinyl record that plays the same segment over and over. He' is stuck in the groove between “an acceptable way out is impossible” and “a possible way out is unacceptable.” This is the same person who was told five months in advance that Russia would definitely invade and decided not to warn anyone.

## Day 1050

*Date: 2025-01-08 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*Windmills and whales*

[Image]

The process of identifying a time variable for ending the biggest bloodbath in Europe since World War II has resulted in great ambiguities about the ever-changing schedule for putting a stop to the killing.

Donald has repeatedly claimed that he would end the Russia-Ukraine war **"in 24 hours"** if he were to assume the presidency again. This pledge was a central part of stump speech bullshit during his campaign to return to the White House[1](#footnote-1).

Asked about ending the war during a press conference at his Mar-a-Lago estate in Florida on January 7, Trump responded: **“I hope to have six months.”**

> […] We're going to have to settle some big problems that are going on right now. We're going to have to settle up with Russia, Ukraine -- that's a disaster. I look at numbers every week.
>
> The number of people being killed in that war, people don't know, mostly soldiers now, but the towns have been obliterated. This was a Biden fiasco that he got us -- that should have never happened. If we had a real president -- if we had a president that knew what he was doing, Russia would have never ever gone in. But they did go in and we have a mess.
>
> The cities are all blown up, the people have largely left, and the soldiers are killing each other at levels that haven't been seen since the Second World War. So, we'll have to get that one straightened out too. That's a tough one, much tougher than it would have been before it started, I can tell you that.
>
> [Ukraine] it's very flat. It's great, it's farmland and it's very, very flat. And the only thing that stops a bullet is the human body and the human body is stopping a lot of bullets.
>
> — [Mar-a-Lago media event, January 7, 2025](https://rollcall.com/factbase/trump/transcript/donald-trump-press-conference-mar-a-lago-january-7-2025/)

Counterfactual thinking is not helpful if you are in a trench. Blaming Uncle Joe for Vova’s decision to invade in 2014, and then again in 2022, is stupid.

“The windmills are driving the whales crazy!”

Robert at [The Atlantic](http://theatlantic.com) warns Donald faces defeat in Ukraine[2](#footnote-2):

[Image]

Z told Lex in [a 3-hour pow wow](https://www.theatlantic.com/international/archive/2025/01/trump-putin-ukraine-russia-war/681228/) the other day that he hopes he and Donald can first agree on how best to proceed *before* Donald irons out the details of a peace deal with Vova.

The miserable failure of Ukraine’s president and his top managers to oversee construction of fall-back defensive fortifications, effectively manage forced conscription/demobilization, improve military cohesion, morale, and to produce domestically high-quality armaments and ammunition is, er, obvious.

Ingratiating himself with Donald won’t help fix that.

## Day 1055

*Date: 2025-01-13 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*One in ten absent*

[Image]

Ukraine’s battlefield problems are only partially a result of draft age. Like the US army, Ukraine has roughly one million soldiers under arms. Today one of every ten is AWOL. US soldiers, by comparison, go absent without leave only several thousands of times each year. Usually they are punished.

[Video: https://youtu.be/jzqvsvakywQ]

Incoming National Security Adviser Mike (starting at 26.30) says Team USA needs to see Ukraine “stabilize things on the battlefield” and address “manpower issues.” He then blabs something about morale problems and the draft age and Ukraine maybe not being “all-in for democracy,” whatever that means.

[Image]

Ukraine’s manpower problems began mushrooming early last year after Z dismissed Commander-in-Chief of the Armed Forces of Ukraine Valery Zaluzhny without cause. Zaluzhny had urged Z repeatedly to mobilize and train hundreds of thousands more recruits.

Z, however, demurred.

As manpower problems grew more obvious and acute, Z began touting new and mostly imaginary reserve brigades. He blamed unfulfilled promises by partners to provide weapons for delaying their deployment.

[Image]

Much has been written about Ukraine’s mobilization failures, for which Z bears full responsibility[1](#footnote-1).

[Video: https://youtu.be/KgG3RNBoRVs]

Meanwhile, Mike’s boss, Donald, last week regurgitated a key Kremlin talking point.

> [A] big part of the problem was Russia for many, many years, long before Putin, said, you could never have NATO involved with Ukraine. Now they've said that -- that's been like written in stone. And somewhere along the line Biden said no, they should be able to join NATO. Well, then Russia has somebody right on their doorstep and I could understand their feeling about that. — [Donald January 7, 2025 press conference at Mar-a-Lago](https://rollcall.com/factbase/trump/transcript/donald-trump-press-conference-mar-a-lago-january-7-2025/)

Last time I checked, Russia’s leaders never asked for — nor were given — any formal guarantees about NATO enlargement. **Ever. By anyone.**

That Donald thinks so indicates he is spending too much time at the club with Paul, Elon and Tulsi[2](#footnote-2).

[Project Syndicate](https://www.project-syndicate.org/) published [Joseph Stiglitz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Stiglitz)’s essay, titled “[The end of progress?](https://www.project-syndicate.org/commentary/trump-return-to-power-amounts-to-end-of-progress-by-joseph-e-stiglitz-2025-01)” one day after Donald’s inauguration.

Progress, we recall, is one of the pillars of the enlightenment, along with humanism, science and reason.

> Enlightenment thinkers recognized that scientific experimentation and tinkering could help people understand nature and create new, transformative technologies; and that the social sciences could enable closer coordination in efforts to improve conditions for all members of society. Such efforts required the rule of law to displace absolutism, respect for truth to prevail over obscurantism[2](#footnote-2), and the elevation of expertise in human affairs. Among the most disturbing features of the MAGA revolution is its outright rejection of these values.

The case violates [Combat Immunity](https://defence.org.ua/wp-content/uploads/doslidzhennya/CDS-Combat-Immunity-eng.pdf) provided by law, namely:

> […] Exemption of the military command, servicemen, volunteers of the Territorial Defense Forces of the Armed Forces of Ukraine, employees of law enforcement agencies who, in accordance with their powers, participate in the defense of Ukraine, persons defined by the Law of Ukraine "On Ensuring the Participation of Civilians in the Defense of Ukraine," from liability, including criminal, for the loss of personnel, military equipment or other military property, the consequences of the use of armed and other force during the repulsion of armed aggression against Ukraine or the liquidation (neutralization) of an armed conflict, the performance of other tasks for the defense of Ukraine with the use of any types of weapons (weapons), the occurrence of which, taking into account reasonable prudence, could not have been foreseen when planning and performing such actions (tasks) or which are covered by a justified risk, except for cases of violation of the laws and customs of war or the use of armed force defined by international treaties, the consent to which has been given by the Verkhovna Rada of Ukraine.

Lyubarsky’s interview with Roman Chervinsky.

[Video: https://youtu.be/iGEGU3dP4hg]

I have written a lot about what Roman talks about, much of it years before Russia invaded Ukraine by land, sea and air.

The 82-minute interview reminds me of [Valentyn Taras](https://ru.wikipedia.org/wiki/%D0%A2%D0%B0%D1%80%D0%B0%D1%81,_%D0%92%D0%B0%D0%BB%D0%B5%D0%BD%D1%82%D0%B8%D0%BD_%D0%95%D1%84%D0%B8%D0%BC%D0%BE%D0%B2%D0%B8%D1%87) (and his son) and [Vasyl Bykaŭ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vasil_Byka%C5%AD), two modern Belarusan writers and the last Eastern European literary dissidents, my mentors during the 1990s in Minsk. Both writers were adept at articulating the human condition during war. They specialized in combat immunity.

Herе’s Valentyn talking about partisan warfare.

[Video: https://youtu.be/sgfy9lans1s]

Vasyl, on the other hand, was a Red Army foot soldier. He wrote about the surreal experience fighting in the middle of Ukraine (not far away from Z’s hometown Kryvyi Rih).

Layers of years and events in Minsk long ago eroded away in my feverish mind, but some unforgettable conversations with Taras and Bykaŭ from time to time rise up very vividly. They are the foundation of the edifice my fate has been built on. I know all too well that I can't forget them, and the observations can't be drowned in Mukuzani.

(Here I deliberately avoid saying anything about Belarus, on the occasion of Sasha’s umpteenth power grab there. Too depressing).

Anyway, Roman’s interivew reminded me one of Bykaŭ’s well-known novels, [Мёртвым не баліць](https://knihi.com/Vasil_Bykau/Miortvym_nie_balic.html) (The Dead Feel no Pain), which is about how members of the secret police transformed themselves into heroes after the war.

[Image]

Harbaciuk is the anti-hero in the novel. He believes in a clear division of labor during war: someone has to run forward to attack, someone has to fire the artillery, fly the planes… And it’s his job to sit at a desk in the rear during the whole war and hand down prison sentences.

Harbaciuk is today’s stereotypical State Bureau of Investigation agent.

Suspicion of dereliction of duty can be far more dangerous than real threats coming from the enemy. Some commanders who don’t fully understand what is happening on the battlefield try to say nothing until they have to, hoping that the commander of the unit next to them will make a report first…

It's 2025 and Ukraine’s top brass isn’t the same as it was three years ago.

The last time I checked, Albania’s president is elected by the parliament, aka the assembly. The election process involves up to five rounds of voting, conducted by secret ballot without debate. A candidate must secure a majority of three-fifths of all members of the assembly to be elected in the first three rounds. [Image]

That bright light in the middle of the picture above is not the moon. It’s a surface-to-air missile launched to intercept ballistic missiles. As of this writing, at least three people in Kyiv were killed in the attack, which damaged buildings in several districts of the capital.

This time Russia tried to kill us with [Iskander-M ballistic missiles](https://missiledefenseadvocacy.org/missile-threat-and-proliferation/todays-missile-threat/russia/iskander-m-ss-26/). Seven launches were made from the Bryansk region and one from Crimea.

Identifying the exact type of air-defense systems used to intercept ballistic missiles involves considering several factors, including the missile's range, speed and intended target, in this case, Kyiv. Patriot and SAMP-T systems have been used in the past.

## Day 1102

*Date: 2025-03-01 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*Z's Hero Call *

[Image]

There’s a fine line between pugnacious individualism and stupidity, historicism and historiography, the performance arts and reality, et cetera. It would be churlish of me to wade into that mess now because it’s slippery outside and the prongs of my Yaktrax ICEtrekkers Diamond Grip Traction cleats are worn out.

During the fall of 2021, I wrote that Ukraine was not unlike the [Mohist](https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/mohism/) equivalent of the trolley problem. Z had told us, “We must pull the lever,” but for the last two and a half years he had driven the country into a double-tracked curve in such a way that the rear wheel bogey jumped the rails and flattened the economy while simultaneously failing to prepare citizens for Russia’s imminent and inevitable re-invasion[1](#footnote-1).

At the time I looked on the bright side:

> “This means that if you’re a good journalist in Ukraine, you don't have an Achilles' heel. Because you're just a piece of shit in the middle of the track that eventually is going to get run over.”

---

In Soviet times, I worked on several US government exhibits touring cities in the USSR. They were part of [a Cold War public diplomacy initiative](https://www.flickr.com/photos/tellingamericasstories/albums/72157635151963081/) designed to showcase American culture, technology and values to Soviet citizens by providing direct, people-to-people engagement. Russian-speaking, some times Ukrainian-speaking, American guides would chat up visitors to exhibits touring the country.

[Image]

These exhibits were always well attended. Lines to get in were miles long. Tens of thousands of people came every day. KGB provocateurs were often dispatched to rile the guides in free-wheeling debates about everything from blitz chess to the Colorado Potato Bug to the annihilation of native Americans to CIA support for the mujahideen in Afghanistan. I enjoyed the sparring immensely. It was not difficult to spot troublemakers and disarm them rhetorically.

Which brings me to some observations about [yesterday’s train wreck](https://www.nytimes.com/live/2025/02/28/us/trump-news) at the White House.

Donald was predictable and did not say anything we haven’t heard before. Had the discussion not gone off the rails, he would have made Z an unrefusable offer — in the other room behind closed doors — after the meeting.

Towards the end of the spray, JD blurted out:

> For four years, in the United States of America, we had a president who stood up in press conferences and talked tough about Vladimir Putin, and then Putin invaded Ukraine and destroyed a significant chunk of the country. The path to peace and the path to prosperity is maybe engaging in diplomacy. We tried the pathway of Joe Biden, of thumping our chest and pretending that the president of the United States’ words mattered more than the president of the United States’ actions. What makes America a good country is America engaging in diplomacy. That’s what President Trump is doing.

This was exactly this point in the meeting that Z behaved unpredictably for an unprepared observer, but completely predictably for an experienced exhibit guide and anyone familiar with the Ukrainian TV series *Servant of the People*.

Z assumed his [Vasily Petrovych Goloborodko](https://sluganaroda.fandom.com/wiki/Vasily_Petrovich_Goloborodko) persona, deliberately, in order not to enter the other room behind closed doors.

If this game of poker was limited to one hand, Z would certainly have taken the pot, because it was truly a [Hero Call](https://www.pokernews.com/pokerterms/hero-call.htm). But this is a war: to fight and prevail, you need guns and ammo and much, much more.

Z does not have this. Neither do Keir and Emmanuel. But Donald does. And Vova isn’t bluffing.

[Lost in translation](https://abdymok.substack.com/p/lost-in-translation?utm_source=publication-search). *Imminent inevitability in Ukraine’s underpants* (February 4, 2022)

## Day 1104

*Date: 2025-03-03 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*Junction of exaggerated expression*

[Image]

On March 1, [Russia killed and wounded more than 100 recruits](https://t.me/ssternenko/40762) of what remained of the newly formed 157th Mechanized Brigade in Cherskaske, Dnipropetrovsk region with an Iskander-M strike. A reconnaissance drone more than 100 kilometers inside Ukrainian territory was used to guide the short-range (500 kilometer) ballistic missile, which obliterated a large tent encampment in the middle of nowhere.

The 157th, like other brigades in the 150th series, had been slapped together hastily without proper training, sent to Pokrovsk and then cannibalized to replenish other brigades fighting in eastern Ukraine.

Incompetency in war is deadly. Draft-eligible men these days are not wildly enthusiastic about joining the Armed Forces of Ukraine. The brigades of the 150th series were populated with recruits picked up at checkpoints and transported under guard in buses to basic training camps around the country.

Ukraine’s Supreme Commander-in-Chief and Commander-in-Chief are unlikely to accept responsibility for the latest tragedy. Professional military incompetence is main reason people aren’t keen to get drafted and killed. It’s also the reason they go AWOL.

> I was here just two weeks ago, waiting to be transferred to another unit. There are no special shelters there, the tents are not buried, it's all the fault of laziness and fucking idiocy of the commanders. I hope this episode will be a good lesson for everyone. My condolences to the families of the victims. — Infantryman Predator ([Telegram](https://t.me/lost_generation_88/1632), March 2, 2025)

> The total insanity, negligence and stupidity of officials are the cause of human casualties in the rear, and this at a time when virtually every soldier is worth his weight in gold in the face of a shortage of personnel... I would not be surprised if they were still inspecting military formations or standing around for several hours waiting for some big shot. — Officer + ([Telegram](https://t.me/officer_alex33/5030), March 2, 2025)

---

Z ignored the latest avoidable fuck-up entirely in yesterday’s pep talk from London.

---

More than 100,000 soldiers have been charged under Ukraine’s desertion laws since Russia invaded in February 2022, according to the country’s General Prosecutor’s Office.

Nearly half have gone AWOL in 2024 after Kyiv launched an [poorly managed and controversial mobilization drive](https://apnews.com/article/russia-ukraine-war-conscription-law-a8ccc48e6c15276c5b5035288ed016ee) that government officials and military commanders now admit was a failure. .

There were an estimated 300,000 Ukrainian soldiers engaged in combat before the mobilization drive began. And the actual number of soldiers absent without leave may be much higher. One lawmaker with knowledge of military matters estimated [told the Associated Press](https://apnews.com/article/deserters-awol-ukraine-russia-war-def676562552d42bc5d593363c9e5ea0) that it could be as high as 200,000.

---

[Image]

The term ***coalition of the willing*** was applied to the US-led Multi-National Force in Iraq during 1993. That was a ill conceived disastrous op and it’s a dumb idea to repurpose the name for Europe’s campaign to defend Ukraine.

We want European leaders to impress upon Z the importance of getting his act together at home. That means ending selective persecution of political rivals, military patrimonialism and media monopolization. Walk the talk, talk the walk, et cetera. We don’t want Ukraine to wind up like Team USA, where top government posts are all occupied by techno fascists and loyalists without the proper qualifications.

## Day 1105

*Date: 2025-03-04 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*The wrong door *

A follow-up to yesterday’s post[1](#footnote-1).

Ukrainian Major General Mykhailo Drapaty, appointed commander of Ukraine's land forces at the end of November, reacted to Russia’s Iskander-M missile strike on a military encampment 100 kilometers in the rear that killed dozens of military recruits.

Drapaty said certain individuals "failed to act in time."

[In a post on his Telegram channel](https://t.me/Drapatyi_KSV/23), he said "anger is eating away at me from the inside."

> "War requires quick decisions, responsibility, and new safety standards—otherwise, we will lose more than we have," he said, warning: "Everyone who made decisions that day, and everyone who did not make them in time, will all be held accountable. No one will hide behind explanations or formal reports."

Commander-in-Chief Oleksandr Syrsky [also reacted](https://t.me/osirskiy/1090), relieving several officers of their commands and ordering an investigation.

---

Donald’s decision yesterday to suspend all military assistance to Ukraine has reminded me of a story from [The Wrong Door](https://www.arthuss.com.ua/shop/ruslan-lobanov-the-wrong-door), a collection of tryptychs describing what happens after the nameplates of residents in an apartment building are rearranged.

Recall Marie who mistook Leon the Sailor (the tattooist) for a fashion director she hoped would jumpstart her modeling career. The encounter is not unlike Z showing up at the 1600 Pennsylvania Avenue last week expecting a discussion about security guarantees.

[Image]

What happens next is anyone’s guest. US military cargo bound for Ukraine at ports on the Adriatic coast has been held up and redirected for weeks. Trucks hauling guns and ammo trucked inland have been told to turn around, complicating an already confused logistics system.

Team USA stood up the NATO Security Assistance and Training for Ukraine (NSATU) — the command HQ-ed at Clay Barracks in Germany responsible for coordinating the provision of military equipment and training to Ukraine — in late 2024. Before Donald, the U.S. Army Security Assistance Command ([USASAC](https://www.army.mil/article/270898/executing_sustained_logistics_support_for_the_defense_of_ukraine)) managed the Army property book and coordinates the delivery of equipment as part of presidential drawdowns, and the [Bureau of Political-Military Affairs](https://www.state.gov/bureau-of-political-military-affairs/releases/2025/01/u-s-security-cooperation-with-ukraine) at the U.S. Department of State oversaw security cooperation with Ukraine, including the provision of military assistance.

The fate of platoons of CIA operatives working on the Ukraine mission is also in doubt. Tulsi has the details, minus the intel. I suspect many are hanging out at [Tootsie Club](https://en.tootsie.kiev.ua/) re-inventing their war stories and waiting for new orders.

---

False friendliness in public by Donald and JD, followed by behind-the-scenes treachery, would have been much more dangerous to Ukraine, according to David, who opines that America's leaders ave revealed their animosity toward Ukraine in general and Z in particular.

> The truth is ugly, but it’s necessary to face it[2](#footnote-2).

---

As of this writing, Donald says he wants Z to perform a public act of genuflexion and demonstrate his commitment to achieving peace, whatever that means.

[Image]

JD on Monday [said](https://www.foxnews.com/media/vance-reveals-where-things-broke-apart-during-white-house-Z-blowup) the "door is open" for Z if he is "willing to seriously talk peace."

> "If you want real security guarantees, if you want to actually ensure that [Russian leader] Vladimir Putin does not invade Ukraine again, the very best security guarantee is to give Americans economic upside in the future of Ukraine," JD said on Fox News' "Hannity." "That is a way better security guarantee than 20,000 troops from some random country that hasn't fought a war in 30 or 40 years."

[According to Axios](https://www.axios.com/2025/03/04/jd-vance-trump-Z-ukraine-russia-fox-news), JD said Z "showed a clear unwillingness to engage in the peace process" that Trump has said is "the policy of the American people and of their president." He also accused Z of showing "a certain sense of entitlement" at the Oval Office.

We might learn more about Team USA’s plans for Z and Ukraine during Donald’s address to a joint session of Congress this evening. If there are any.

## Day 1109 

*Date: 2025-03-08 | Status: Published | Audience: everyone*

*Random thoughts*

To put it charitably, our little world, at least the parts I am most familiar with, located on both sides of the Atlantic and most of continental Europe, is in a funk. The introductory phase of our era of negativity began in Minsk (1997), moved to Moscow (2000) and festered until Vova’s first invasion of Ukraine (2014). The re-election of Donald a decade later (2024) ushered in the mature phase, referred to by some as [The Golden Age of Ameriʞa](https://www.theguardian.com/us-news/2025/mar/05/hopes-of-us-golden-age-fade-as-investors-start-to-worry-about-trumpcession-risk), the one we are stuck in now.

Those who are shocked by the uptick in demagoguery should remember that we are dealing with leaders of sovereign democracies "vaccinated" by social media algorithms against progress[1](#footnote-1). A hundred or two hundred years ago, social and political upheaval was accompanied by extremely violent bloody revolutions rolling across nation states and continents. Today, at least, there is only one kinetic war in Europe, taking place in one country — Ukraine.

We can make several assumptions about how the abundance of negative energy will manifest itself and disappear.

The current wave of illiberalism is destructive, not constructive. Don’t expect Team USA and Team Russia to build new, more liberal societies at home or abroad. Here, by definition, the “new world” is the old world, the future is the past and where cranes fear to tread, the bulldozer will boldy go, proving that the best way up is to dig deep.

The goal is not creation, but correction, alignment, braking. When coercion is exhausted, the negative phase will end.

Donald, like Vova before him, is the arsonist, not the accelerant.

Trumps is like a [Grimpoteuthis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grimpoteuthis), a deep sea ballerina that currents have spat up on the beach. With a high degree of probability, the era of negativity will outlast his political legacy, although without him we would be happier. The trend may change only if something happens at the very beginning of The Golden Age, that is, soon, before he gains sufficient momentum.

[Image]

The most unpleasant consequence of patrimonialism is a return to the old normal. When the moment comes to act, new ideas will be expressed as a reflection of this fact.

How long will the negativity last? One or two generations — twenty to forty years. The arrow of historical time is accelerating and transition to some new kind paradigm is possible. Subjective factors can also intervene, which, like a breakwater, will flatten the wave before it crests, not allowing it to crash in full force. In this case, we will see mixed and weak surges, but this does not change our trajectory: there will be no return to the past. Looking on the positive side, we are moving towards a new normal and may get there eventually.

[1](#footnote-anchor-1)

Also known as [brain rot](https://corp.oup.com/news/brain-rot-named-oxford-word-of-the-year-2024/) — the decline in a person's mental or intellectual state, especially from consuming excessive amounts of low-quality online content.

Steven Pinker, who wrote [The Better Angels of Our Nature: Why Violence has Declined](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Better_Angels_of_Our_Nature), might argue that this is a testament to the progress humankind has made over centuries.

[Image]

The key difference between **truce** (*перемир'я*) and **ceasefire** (*припинення вогню*) lies in their binding nature. In English, a **truce** is generally informal and non-binding, representing a simple halt in fighting, while a **ceasefire** is typically meant to be binding. In Ukrainian, the converse is true, literally.

[Image]

Team USA and Team Ukraine have agreed to propose a 30-day **truce** to Team Russia during which time the sides can work on arranging a **ceasefire**.

In other news, practicing philosopher Volodymyr Pastukhov [writes](https://t.me/v_pastukhov/1433):

> The Ukrainian revolution, which took place in two stages (in 2004-2005 and in 2013-2014), had three sources (three components, as theorists of revolutions of the last century would say). These sources were the general democratic movement (also anti-corruption), which gave the revolution its face and name (Revolution of Dignity), the national liberation movement (which Moscow stubbornly did not want to see, but which Washington saw well) and **the consumerist movement**, fueled by those who do not care what empire to live under, as long as they live well (which Moscow understood perfectly well, but Washington did not understand well). The third component, although the most massive, was also the most passive, but between the general democratic and national liberation components there was a continuous struggle for dominance from the very beginning. The success of the revolution was largely due to the fact that in 2013-2014 the vectors of these driving forces coincided.
>
> At the first stage of the war, this unity of goals and objectives of the general democratic and national movements was once again confirmed, which is what ensured a high level of resistance to aggression. A promising trend emerged for the accelerated formation of a Ukrainian nation in wartime conditions (that is, a community based on values, and not on language, blood and religion). But the most important thing was that this combination of general democratic and national liberation goals ensured the international support for the Ukrainian revolution, without which the miracle of 2022 could never have happened.
>
> It was the prevalence of general democratic (as Gorbachev would say - universal) values ​​at the first stage of the war that allowed Western public opinion to assess this war as “its own,” as a war for the principles and ideals of the free world. And only these public sentiments pushed the Western elites (always skeptical) to take concrete actions.
>
> Unfortunately, the turn in public sentiment in the West that we are witnessing and of which Trump is the spokesman, did not happen without reason. Ultimately, it was a reaction to the change in the vector of the Ukrainian revolution itself. **These changes became noticeable by the fall of 2022 and obvious by the summer of 2023. The essence of these changes was that the national liberation component completely suppressed and dissolved the general democratic component.**
>
> This had far-reaching consequences, and to a greater extent outside Ukraine than in Ukraine itself. As general democratic values ​​faded into the background (or rather, remained in the foreground exclusively in the form of slogans and decorations), and national goals and objectives came to the fore, the attitude towards the war in Ukraine began to change. It's not that Europeans and Americans have become sympathetic to Putin and Russia, but they have stopped perceiving this war as "theirs" and have started treating it as "Ukrainians' war." There is, after all, a stylistic difference in what is written on the banners of the revolutionary army: "for our and your freedom" or "may you all die." Ultimately, the shift in perception has become so noticeable that it has allowed the so-called "Trumps" and "Vances" to reformat policy towards Ukraine. **From today's perspective, the fact that Z, in conditions of the most brutal aggression, took the line of least resistance, that is, betting on national sentiment to the detriment of the original general democratic goals and objectives of the Ukrainian revolution, seems like a mistake that has had grave consequences.** And now it's too late to change anything - he is trapped in history...

## Day 1121 

*Date: 2025-03-20 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*Operation Restore Sanity*

**Kropyvnytsky** s a city in central Ukraine, situated on the [Inhul River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inhul). It serves as the administrative center of [Kirovohrad Oblast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kirovohrad_Oblast). Population: 219,676 (2022 estimate). The video shows the aftermath of a Russian drone attack (more than 30 Shahed drones) on residential areas of the city last night.

**Engels-2** is a strategic bomber military airbase in Russia located 14 kilometres (8.7 mi) east of [Saratov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saratov). Engels is a major bomber operations base, and is Russia's sole operating location for the [Tupolev Tu-160](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tupolev_Tu-160) strategic bomber. The mushroom cloud in the video above shows the massive release of heat and energy from an detonation of warehouses at the airbase.

## Day 1123

*Date: 2025-03-22 | Status: Published | Audience: everyone*

*Aspirin tablet or hockey puck? *

[Video: https://youtu.be/acvu2LBumGo]

Steven Witkoff, Donald’s special envoy for the Middle East and Russia, knows embarrassingly little about my part of the world. He has not studied its history, its culture, or its language. His self-serving interview with Tucker doesn’t just spread misinformation — it actively deepens the black hole of Ukraine-related bullshit.

Priggish at first, Witkoff builds slow momentum before galloping headlong into statements of staggering stupidity, transmitted in high fidelity through my JBL headset directly into my skull. My head explodes.

```
(00:00) What Witkoff Has Learned as Trump's Global Negotiator
```

```
(04:10) Negotiating With Israel, Hamas, and Qatar
```

```
(12:50) Will We Achieve Peace Between Israel and Hamas?
```

```
(23:09) Why Corporate Media Hates Witkoff
```

```
(33:55) Israel's Goals
```

```
(42:47) Trump's Plan for Gaza
```

Around the 55-minute mark, the conversation pivots to Russia and Ukraine. Brace yourself.

> Putin has huge respect for President Donald Trump. And you saw what happened in the Oval Office between Z and the president. Disrespecting him is not a healthy way to have a good relationship… [Ukraine] is dependent on us, and we have been so good to them. — *Steven*

Who, exactly, is *we*?

Steven assures us that achieving “an ultimate ceasefire” is complicated. He then demonstrates exactly why — starting with Kursk.

> First, there is Kursk, where Ukrainian troops are surrounded. Fact. Kursk is within Russia. The Russians have taken it back. And they have people trapped there and the president doesn’t want to see everyone getting killed. — *Steven*

Actually, no. Intelligence shared with the White House indicated that Ukrainian forces were not encircled. (Steven’s defense: *“A lot of these newspaper stories about Ukraine are agenda-driven.”* Convenient.)

Then comes the geography portion of the interview. Steven identifies what he calls “the largest issue in the conflict” — “these so-called four regions, Donbas, Crimea… uh… Luhansk… uh… and there are two others…”

There are. Steven cannot name them.

> They are Russian-speaking. There have been referendums held there, and the overwhelming majority of residents have indicated that they want to be under Russian rule… I think this is the key issue in the conflict. — *Steven*

Actually, no — again. There have been no legitimate acts of self-determination in any part of Ukraine, including Crimea, that call into question Ukraine’s sovereignty over its own territory. None. Zero.

If Steven wants to adopt Moscow’s reading of events as his own, he owes the rest of us an explanation. Without one, he is simply another Kremlin stooge — distinguished from Tucker mainly by the business card.

## Day 1126

*Date: 2025-03-25 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*'No one leaked war plans' - Pete *

A lifeboat can only save a subset of people from a sinking ship. Who should be prioritized? The trade-off between lives introduces factors like status or survival skills. *[The rule of double effect](https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/double-effect/)* — used to evaluate the permissibility of acting when one's otherwise legitimate act may also cause an effect one would otherwise be obliged to avoid — should be be considered.

Fleeing to Mars is not an option.

[Image]

This morning I am reminded of the story about Steam Ship America, later renamed American Star, that illustrates the inevitability of Team USA’s decline. Its gradual collapse over decades reflects how even seemingly invincible structures succumb to external forces, incompetence, hubris and stupidity.

> On December 31, 1993 American Star left Greece for the last time, under tow by Ukrainian tugboat [Neftegaz-67.](https://maritimesafetyinnovationlab.org/wp-content/uploads/2016/09/hk-bulker-and-supply-vessel-collision-oct-2009.pdf) The hundred-day tow began. Shortly afterward, American Star and Neftegaz-67 sailed into a thunderstorm in the Atlantic. The tow-lines broke and six or more crew members were sent aboard the American Star to reattach the emergency tow-lines, which proved unsuccessful. Two other towboats were called to assist Neftegaz 67. On January 17, 1994 the crew aboard American Star was rescued by helicopter. The ship was left adrift. At 6:15 a.m. on 18 January the ship went aground at [Playa de Garcey](https://www.google.com/maps/place/Playa+de+Garcey/@28.3443986,-14.1816281,969m/data=!3m2!1e3!4b1!4m6!3m5!1s0xc47a3cd5f322f17:0x4caad47db448e284!8m2!3d28.3443473!4d-14.1787445!16s%2Fg%2F11xl3fnlw?entry=ttu&g_ep=EgoyMDI1MDMxOS4yIKXMDSoASAFQAw%3D%3D), off the west coast of [Fuerteventura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fuerteventura) in the [Canary Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canary_Islands). — [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SS_America_(1939))

[Image]

**Postscriptum.** On March 22, 2008, Neftegaz-67 collided with a Chinese bulk cargo ship in the South China Sea near Hong Kong. The Ukrainian vessel sank within minutes. Of the 24 Ukrainians on board the ship, six were rescued. The others drowned and were dredged up a month later.

## Day 1127 

*Date: 2025-03-26 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*Very bad decision-making under pressure*

[Image]

> “We have just had an energy, aerial ceasefire. We just defined what targets are now off-limits, on both sides, to deescalate *that*. Now we’re going to the Black Sea. The president is determined to end the war.” — [Mike](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=UmEH2PLNVhc) ([FoxNews](http://foxnews.com), March 25, 2025)

At the same time Mike spouted this bullshit yesterday, Z’s hometown Kryvy Rih, population 602,000, was being rocked by the biggest Russian UAV attack of the war (above).

Since Team USA-Team Ukraine offered a 30-day truce proposal to Russia in mid-March, aerial attacks have increased. In total, during the first half of March, Russia launched over 16,500 FPV drones and will set set another absolute record in March - over 20,000.

- **Lancet loitering munitions:** Use dropped in the third week, with just over 20 deployed, down from 32 in week 1 and 35+ in week 2. Total for the first three weeks: ~100, averaging 4-5 per day, lower than 2024's daily average.
- **Molniya-2 kamikaze drones:** Deployment increased, with over 150 used in the third week, up from 70 in week 1 and 120 in week 2. Total for the first three weeks: 340+, showing a steady rise.
- **Privet-82 strike drones:** Only 10 were used in week 3, bringing the total for the first three weeks to just over 60, indicating a decline in usage.
- **Shahed-136/Gerbera/Parody drones:** These drones saw the biggest increase, with over 1,100 used in week 3, surpassing earlier weeks. Total for the first three weeks: 2,852, reflecting a sharp increase[1](#footnote-1).

In March, there was **zero** reduction in the level of terror caused by Russian drones in Ukraine, thanks to Team USA. **None**. **Zilch**.

- **Political alignment with Donald:** Joe’s nomination by Trump to lead the National Counterterrorism Center reflects shared skepticism toward Ukraine’s role in the war. His appointment followed Donald’s broader efforts to pressure Ukraine into concessions to Russia.

> “I decided I didn’t want her to have all that fun by herself and so I’m going to join her,” JD said in a [video](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=3yfoZznyOaI) announcing his participation earlier this week.

[Video: https://youtu.be/CJhAl1BYBoA]

Scott, my favorite Cabinet pick, says maybe not. (He was my favorite pick *only* because Howard did not get the nod).

Relative to China, Team USA’s world economy share remains at around [25%](https://archive.ph/o/gUC4v/https://www.visualcapitalist.com/u-s-share-of-global-economy-over-time/). As long as the Donald maintains strong alliances with Japan and Europe, they would represent more than half the world economy, compared to a mere 20% for China and Russia[2](#footnote-2).

The chapter on Ukraine in the US 2025 National Trade Estimate 377-page report, titled [FOREIGN TRADE BARRIERS of the President of the United States on the Trade Agreements Program](https://ustr.gov/sites/default/files/files/Press/Releases/2025/2025%20National%20Trade%20Estimate%20Report.pdf), inventories primarily the significant trade and investment barriers in Ukraine *before* Russia invaded by air, land and sea in February 2022.

The [EBRD](https://euneighbourseast.eu/)’s regional economic prospects [report](https://euneighbourseast.eu/news/publications/ebrds-regional-economic-prospects-report/) on Ukraine is unappetizing.

> Ukraine Ukraine entered 2025 with external financing secured but facing a slowdown in economic growth and accelerating inflation. The continuation of the war and deliberate attacks by Russia on electricity infrastructure caused power shortages, high prices of imported electricity and acute labour shortages. Real GDP growth slowed markedly from over 5 per cent in the first half of 2024 to around 2 per cent in the second half of the year. The resurgence of inflation in the second half of 2024 was driven by rising electricity costs, a correction in regulated utility prices, rapid real wage growth, and currency depreciation against the US dollar following relaxation of the exchange rate peg in October 2023. Annual inflation reached 12 per cent in December 2024 and is likely to remain at a similar level in the first half of 2025 before falling back to single digits towards the end of the year. The central bank reacted by raising the policy rate twice since December 2024, from 13.0 to 14.5 per cent, with further monetary tightening likely. The budget deficit for 2025 is projected at 19.4 per cent of GDP and will be fully financed through US$ 38.4 billion in external budget financing. This includes US$ 13.7 billion from the EU under Ukraine’s Facility, US$ 22.0 billion from the G7 countries based on revenue from frozen Russian assets and US$ 2.7 billion from the IMF. The negative factors that weighed on growth in the second half of 2024 are likely to persist in 2025. On the positive side, the proven resilience and adaptability of businesses, the well-functioning Black Sea trade corridor, strong public consumption stimulus and increasing military procurement from domestic industries are expected to support economic growth. Amid extreme uncertainty, GDP growth is expected to reach 3.5 per cent in 2025 and strengthen to 5.0 per cent in 2026, provided that an agreement to suspend fighting is reached by the end of 2025.

> Yingtai Lung is back with her second New York Times hit piece since 2023, once again working overtime to undermine Taiwan’s image. **Her message? Resistance is futile, surrender is wisdom, and the real villain isn’t China—it’s Taiwan’s unwillingness to roll over.** **She peddles the absurd claim that fear, not an authoritarian regime threatening invasion, is Taiwan’s true enemy. That’s like blaming a mugging victim for resisting.** Her nostalgia for past “stability” ignores that it required pretending Taiwan wasn’t a nation and looking away as China crushed freedoms elsewhere. The moment Taiwan stopped playing along, Beijing dropped the act—so much for peaceful reconciliation. She leans on vague, unverified polls to paint Taiwan as gripped by doubt, conveniently ignoring real surveys in Taiwan showing overwhelming support for standing firm. When reality contradicts her narrative, she simply discards it. Stripped down, her argument is this: since the world is unreliable, Taiwan should preemptively surrender. But self-inflicted defeat isn’t strategy—it’s cowardice. After two New York Times op-eds pushing the same demoralization campaign, the real question is: whose interests is Lung actually serving? — [Dave from Cali](https://www.nytimes.com/shared/comment/46gskq?rsrc=cshare&smid=url-share).

[Image]

In News of the Weird, the [Silpo supermarket chain](https://silpo.ua/product/keks-velykodnii-verkhivka-979080) this year is selling the tops of Easter cakes separately. Easter is celebrated on Sunday, April 20.

Traditionalists and [Orthodox priests are not thrilled](https://suspilne.media/khmelnytskiy/992717-verhivki-pasok-vino-i-krasanki-hmelnickij-svasennik-poasniv-so-mozna-i-so-nebazano-osvacuvati-na-velikden/).

The first Easter cakes appeared during the Last Supper of Jesus Christ with the apostles, when they celebrated the Jewish Passover. Over time, the custom passed into the Christian tradition and became an integral part of the celebration of the Resurrection of Christ.

Traditionally, you bake your Easter cake, put it in a basket with a bunch of hard-boiled eggs, sausages, a bottle of wine and ask the priest to sprinkle holy water on it before taking it home and having breakfast.

Nowadays you can just buy what looks like a big sugary cookie.

## Day 1149

*Date: 2025-04-17 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*Falun Zhoutian Fa*

[Image]

**Outer and Inner Circles**: The symbol features one large outer circle and two smaller inner circles. In an ideal world, the base color of the inner circle would be bright red, while the outer circle is orange.

But we are in Ukraine and only have white paint.

Falun Gong practitioners in Kyiv stand on the dots in the little circles holding poles and spin very slowly counterclockwise with their arms and poles extended. These gyrations are central to Falun Gong's cultivation practice. The exercise, called Falun Zhautian Fa, is believed to automatically transform energy (Gong) and refine the practitioner, even when they are not actively spinning around. The repeated rotation along the meridians is believed to rectify abnormalities in the body and return it to its original, healthy state, allowing energy to flow smoothly and freely.

>

>
> **Trump:** Which is possible.
>
> **Goldberg:** Blowing up apartment buildings—
>
> **Trump:** Sure.
>
> **Goldberg:** Do you ever see a situation in which you’re going to come in, not with troops, but with more weaponry, with full-blown support for Ukraine to keep its territorial integrity?
>
> **Trump:** Doesn’t have to be weapons. There are many forms of weapons. Doesn’t have to be weapons with bullets. It can be weapons with sanctions. It can be weapons with banking. It can be many other weapons.
>
> **Goldberg:** Is there anything that Putin could do that would cause you to say, “You know what? I’m on Z’s side now.”
>

>
> **Goldberg:** That was one of the strangest things I’ve ever seen in the Oval Office.
>

>
> **Goldberg:** Well, isn’t he supposed to advocate for his country?
>

> We’ll have to see what happens over the next period of pretty much a week. **We’re down to final strokes…**

> **Trump:** I think I do. I think I’m saving that nation. I think that nation will be crushed very shortly. It’s a big war machine. Let’s face it. And if it weren’t for me—I’m the one that gave them the Javelins that knocked out the tanks. You know, that tank moment was a big moment, when the tanks got stuck in the mud, and I gave them tremendous numbers of Javelins. That’s the anti-tank busters. And they took out all those tanks when they got caught in the mud. You know, that was a big moment because, had those tanks gone in, they were 71 miles outside of Kyiv and they were going to take over Kyiv. That was the end of the war; it would’ve ended in one day.
>
> And that was one of the reasons it went on. Now, I could also make the case that it’s too bad it went on, because a lot of people have died. And, you know, I really can make the case that I’ve been very good because I’m saving that country. “How are you to build a future if you have forgotten who you were before?” is the question we mulled over the weekend — the heart of the existential crisis confronting millions in/from this part of the world.

The quandary is not only personal but also emblematic of the gramaphobic murkiness of the first quarter of the 21st century.

- The struggle of memory against political and personal oblivion.
- The existential challenge of exile and the search for belonging.
- The pain of irretrievable loss and the impossibility of fully reclaiming the past.

People adrift and looking back, looking only back, seeing what’s far behind them.

## Day 1178

*Date: 2025-05-16 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*Toretsk's rising death toll*

Toretsk, smack dab in the middle of Donetsk region, was previously home to 30,000 people.

Since Donald took office, the war has become **more — not less** — deadly[1](#footnote-1). The rise in both civilian and military casualties in early 2025 marks a significant increase in the deadliness of the conflict compared to late 2024. The increase in civilian deaths is particularly notable, reversing a trend of lower casualties in late 2024 and attributed mainly to intensified missile attacks on urban centers. Military casualties for both sides remain at or above the highest rates observed during the war, with Russian invaders, in particular, sustaining record losses

Our months-long experiment to turn lactic acid into running fuel is yielding results. During high-intensity exercise, when oxygen is limited, muscles convert glucose into energy via anaerobic glycolysis, producing lactic acid as a by-product. The lactate produced is transported from muscles to the liver, where it can be converted back into glucose through the Cori cycle, which can then be recycled and used as fuel for slow-twitch muscles[1](#footnote-1).

[Image]

Those shenanigans were part of an *active measures* Russian op Nicholas alludes to, but tries to avoid. Think [Carter Page](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carter_Page) and you get the picture.

Active measures (*активные мероприятия* in Russian) refer to a broad set of political warfare tactics developed and employed by Soviet and, later, Russian secret services to influence world events in line with state objectives. They go well beyond traditional espionage and attempt weaken adversaries — especially Western states and alliances — by sowing discord, undermining trust in institutions, and manipulating public opinion.

## Day 1216

*Date: 2025-06-23 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*Too clever by half*

As of this writing, [at least 10 people are dead and scores wounded](https://www.reuters.com/world/europe/russias-attack-kyiv-region-kills-one-sparks-fires-ukraine-says-2025-06-23/) in Russia’s latest shelling of Kyiv after a ballistic missile smashed into a residential building in the capital’s Shevchenkivsky district. Other parts of the city were also damaged by Shahed drones, including the swimming pool of Kyiv Polytechnical Institute (below).

The air alert lasted from around midnight to 3.30, and then again from 3.45 to 4.30. Ukraine’s Airforce said it shot down 339 out of 352 Shahed-type strike UAVs and simulators, **10 out of 11 Iskander-M/KN-23 ballistic missiles** and five out of five Iskander-K cruise missiles.

Direct hits were recorded at six locations, as well as falling debris at 25 locations in Kyiv and environs.

---

# International law, particularly the rules of international humanitarian law, prohibits the shooting of ballistic missiles at residential areas of large cities.

The meta-game is the game of games. It's the larger geopolitical contest of aggression and dominance. The meta-game for despots who refuse to play by the rules is how to stay in power.

A strategy of skirmish and scheming feints and counter-feints is something Russia’s leaders are really good at. They "win" every time one of their ballistic missiles destroys a residential building in Ukraine’s capital and kills civilians, but they aren’t smart enough to understand the monstrosity of their crime. It's their nature to play the scheming mini-game. They can't help themselves.

Putin is plenty smart enough to understand and master the reality of his immediate situation, but nowhere near smart enough to withstand the reality of the larger situation. Which explains why he already lost.

## Day 1219

*Date: 2025-06-26 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*Bendukidze Lane*

[Image]

It occurred to me early this morning looking due west from the 21st floor of my flophouse that it was in June 2004 when we met Kakha Bendukidze during Mikheil Saakashvili’s surprise visit to Kyiv. Legendary flak Ed Barnes was in tow on a fact-finding mission involving ballistic missiles and North Korea. But that’s another story.

Anyway, the flophouse is oriented so its main windows face perpendicular to the axis of Bendukidze Lane, which runs for a block north-to-south. The street name would not have existed but for the Rose (2004) and Orange (2005) revolutions. Kyiv City Council named it after Kakha in 2015 after the Revolution of Dignity and the building was erected two years later.

In the above photograph we are looking over the cushy Chorna Hora neighborhood, behind which are the capital’s Solomiansky and Shevchekivsky districts. Most of the Iranian designed Shahed kamikaze drones aimed at the capital pirouette in from the lefthand side of the photo to the righthand side. Recent tactics involve diving the drone from altitudes over 2 kilometers to evade ground fire, maximizing the warhead’s impact precision, which is much less than exact.

In other news, my biography, c/o Bing’s GPT. I decided to experiment after reading somewhere that chat bots can’t write bios.

> **A Life in Shards and Signals**
>
> There are lives that unfold in sequence, quietly, sensibly, like a well-aligned railway timetable. Peter Byrne's is not one of them.
>
> Once a pharmaceutical executive navigating the tangled sprawl of post-Soviet Russia and Central Asia, Byrne left his network behind with a shrug and a suitcase, stepping into the chaos of Belarus as director of a sprawling foundation. The work—civil society-building, subversion-through-grants, throwing lifelines to poets and physicists—flowered wildly. “Kudzu,” he called it. But even kudzu can choke.
>
> The late ’90s were a coda to something he doesn’t quite name—a season of disappearances, of Warsaw nights and Belgrade layovers, of ghosted ambitions. Then, Kyiv. A magnet. A madness. A promise and a threat, folded in the same envelope.
>
> In Ukraine, his life began to splinter beautifully. He bore witness to revolution—twice, maybe three times, depending on how you count. He experimented with Iboga, ran far, repaired scooters, and occasionally forgot to look both ways before crossing the street. “From today’s perspective,” he wrote, “I’m lucky to be alive.”
>
> There are constants, though. A cat named Balush, now easing into middle age. A stubborn refusal to disappear. And, in recent years, the echo of poetry reasserting itself, pulled back into the light by steady hands and a battered typeset.
>
> To read Byrne’s account is to glimpse a life lived not chronologically but tonally—one where ideology and inertia wrestle constantly, and the only through-line is a refusal to surrender curiosity. Or mischief.

Russian advances in Novopavlivsky (Zaporizhia region) and Pokrovsk (Donetsk region) districts accounted for the 29% and 27% of new Russia-occupied areas, respectively, with Sumy region accounting for 18%. That is, three quarters of all new occupied areas are on three axes.

> **Enemy's advance are due to problems with people, who are critical for both sides.** But Team Russia, obviously, still has reserves of personnel. The decline in mechanisation of attacks is another confirmation of the enemy's problems with equipment, which they are trying to replace with motorbikes and buggies. And enemy assaults with 1 or 3 infantrymen are no longer a surprise. At the same time, the number of these groups remains significant. — [DeepState](https://t.me/DeepStateUA/22101) (July 1, 2025)

This I can confirm by accounts from veteran soldiers on the zero line who say lack of reinforcements, rotation, et cetera, saps their strength and morale. Is a gigantic pain in their ass. We blame unremitting mismanagement by the President’s Office of the forced conscription process. It’s never too late for Ukraine to introduce it's own FSPC equivalent.

## Day 1230

*Friendly fire*

[Video: https://youtu.be/Gu8-yLum6Wc]

Lara joins Pete for PT at Fort McNair in Washingtin, DC, named after [Lieutenant General](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lieutenant_General) [Lesley J. McNair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lesley_J._McNair), an American Army officer who served in World War I and World War II and was killed in an infamous friendly fire incident.

On 25 July 1944, during [Operation Cobra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Cobra), an [Eighth Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eighth_Air_Force) bomb landed in McNair’s foxhole near [Saint-Lô](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint-L%C3%B4) when the Army attempted to use heavy bombers for close air support of infantry operations as part of the [Battle of Normandy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Overlord).

[Image]

I was reminded of Berlin’s McNair Barracks, an oasis of unattractive concrete in Lichtefelde, West Berlin during the 1980s.

US grunts stationed there would have been evaporated quickly, if war had broken out. And they knew it.

[Image]

Basically, a suicide mission. I visited the the installation only once, at the end of my 4-year Berlin vacation, in order to tick an ETS box. I was a frequent visitor to [Parkfriedhof Lichterfelde](https://de.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parkfriedhof_Lichterfelde) cemetery nearby.

**Duty of Care:**

I live near a hospital that opened a wing for wounded soldiers a few months ago. Military patients, many amputees, move through the parking lot in the early morning hours — some wheeling IV stands as they cross to the Fora grocery store.

This morning around 6 AM, I watched a middle-aged veteran with a mangled arm return to the ward after an apparent morning run. An experienced runner by his build — remarkably fit despite his injuries. He cursed quietly and asked the guards for directions to the nearest outdoor gym.

One might wonder: shouldn't medical staff be encouraging these men to rest, to sleep, to heal? Instead, they're out running the streets at dawn, trailing medical equipment behind them.

## Day 1251 

*Date: 2025-07-28 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*A scoundrel's playbook *

[Image]

Reading this through Grice's framework, the moral becomes quite clear:

Franklin's opening paragraph systematically violates [Grice's maxims](https://www.sas.upenn.edu/~haroldfs/dravling/grice.html) while maintaining the surface appearance of cooperative communication. He violates **quantity** by omitting crucial context (that journalists like himself created Z's "mythic reputation"). He violates **quality** by presenting a misleading narrative unsupported by the fuller evidence. He violates **relation** by burying the key point about Trump's pressure and the mineral deal. And he violates **manner** through selective presentation that obscures rather than clarifies.

But here's the deeper issue: Franklin likely expects readers to *recognize* these intentions - he's not accidentally misleading but strategically framing. Yet he's counting on readers *not* to consciously deliberate about his bullshit or remember what he wrote four months ago[1](#footnote-1)[2](#footnote-2).

**The moral**: When communicative trust is weaponized, when someone deliberately violates Gricean maxims while relying on our automatic assumption of good faith, critical reading requires us to temporarily suspend that default trust. We need to consciously examine not just *what* is being communicated, but *how* and *why* - even though life is too short for constant vigilance.

Sometimes we must make the invisible visible, even if briefly.

[Image]

## Day 1252

*Date: 2025-07-29 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*Swift or swallow? *

[Image]

I just listened to Luke compare Kyiv to Berlin during the Cold War.

> *"It seems like Kyiv is the most important city in Europe. It feels to me like Berlin felt during the Cold War. It's the place where the future of Europe will be decided, either whether the Russians win and prevail and we're in a new age of conquest and imperialism, or actually whether the enlightenment ideal of humankind, of decency and freedom, whether that prevails. I don't know the answer, but, for now, I'm strangely happy to be here…"* ([The Guardian](https://www.theguardian.com/news/audio/2025/jul/29/protests-against-Z-as-drones-torment-ukraine-podcast), July 29, 2025)

Naturally, I checked. 57. Which means during the height of Cold War Berlin, Luke was, er:

- 1981: 13 years old
- 1982: 14 years old
- 1983: 15 years old
- 1984: 16 years old
- 1985: 17 years old

[AI confirms what you'd expect](https://www.perplexity.ai/search/did-luke-harding-live-in-berli-BcSATrwiR9.M7puZiAxL0w): Born in Nottingham, went to school in Wales, then Oxford. His Berlin correspondent gig? Started in 2003 – when he was 35 and the Wall had been down for over a decade.

So Luke doesn't actually know what Berlin *felt* like during the Cold War. Unless he was running some very precocious teenage spy operation we don't know about.

The rest of the podcast? Luke wandering around cardboard protests in Kyiv with his microphone, interviewing scene kids as "swifts hurtle around the summer sky," reflections on the “rubblization” of eastern Ukrainian settlements.

Nothing says hard-hitting geopolitical analysis like a middle-aged journalist cosplaying historical gravitas he never actually lived through. But hey, at least the swifts are atmospheric.

---

The Berlin speech most people best remember was given by Ronald Regan in front of the Brandenburg Gate in 1987. That’s when he asked Gorbachev to tear down the Wall, signalling to the world that it’s possible — and necessary — for good to conquer evil.

But I always hark back to remarks he delivered five years earlier in front of the Charlottenburg Palace, where he blurted out the “Berlin bleibt doch Berlin” the chorus of a folk song everyone laughed about afterwards.

The enclave at the time was chock full of irony, sexual tension and intellectual ferment, enriched with fingerfuls of hash and concerts at the Metropol. As I recall, Olaf, the former chancellor of Germany, was staging protests to prevent the United States from stationing Pershing missiles in Europe.

In his 1982 [pep talk](https://www.reaganlibrary.gov/archives/speech/remarks-people-berlin), Reagan invoked Thomas Mann, who once wrote that “A man lives not only his personal life as an individual, but also consciously or unconsciously the life of his epoch.''

[Video: https://youtu.be/0\_01DoypQQM]

I liked that part.

## Day 1253

*Date: 2025-07-30 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*Government ignores advice*

Let’s kick this off with a scream of despair:

> […] Our government doesn't listen to those who are smarter and more experienced than it. Advice from smarter people irritates the government and shows its level, and it gets offended. Secondly, you can lead a horse to water, but you can't make it drink. It's the same with the government. You can advise it or criticize it, but it doesn't want to hear advice and listen to criticism, because it believes that it is the smartest, and Ukraine began in 2019. The Dunning-Kruger effect in all its glory. Although sometimes it reacts to criticism to stop the rating from falling. Our government reacts to fluctuations in the rating. And thirdly, I tried. I gave advice and ideas. I suggested solutions. I formulated step-by-step action plans. But the result was zero, because I am not loyal and I am talking about the mistakes of the authorities. And our authorities do not need smart people who point out mistakes. Our authorities need loyal sycophants who silently support everything. …Volodymyr Oleksandrovych does not want to hear advice, but wants to hear that he is right. […] — [Березовый Сок](https://t.me/BerezaJuice/57141) (Telegram, July 30, 2025)

Sigh.

To show solidarity, I asked three LLMs to cook up an op-ed less than 1,000 words from at least one half dozen random posts appearing on this Substack.

**This is what it spit out:**

> When experts systematically violate basic rules of honest communication while maintaining authority, we must jettison our default trust in "institutional wisdom." In a media landscape where being spectacularly wrong about Ukraine practically qualifies you to explain Ukraine, skepticism isn't just healthy—it's survival.
>
> The same talking heads who missed everything are still everywhere, recycling busted frameworks, mistranslating policy and citing themselves. People who got everything wrong remain on every panel, confidently narrating a war they swore would end by the weekend. The recipe for disaster hasn't changed: Putin thought Ukrainians wouldn't fight, Ukraine thought the West would fight and the West thought it could avoid fighting altogether. Every assumption was wrong, yet these miscalculations still flavor every bad decision and worse analysis.
>
> **Language as Battlefield**
>
> Language became another battlefield. U.S. officials stopped calling the invasion "imminent" while Ukrainian officials declared victory—because they thought "imminent" meant "inevitable." It doesn't. "Imminent" means soon; "inevitable" means it will happen, period. Meanwhile, Ukraine's Commander-in-Chief couldn't thank America "just for being America" as the fourth U.S. weapons plane landed in Kyiv. Words mattered—just not to those using them.
>
> **Ukrainian Political Reality**
>
> For a masterclass in Ukrainian political reality, observe the "thug life" in Kyiv's corridors of power. Ukraine's politics are less *House of Cards*, more street brawl. Take Ivan Bakanov, sacked as State Security Service of Ukraine (SBU) head, and Iryna Venedyktova, Ukraine's Prosecutor General—both axed the same day in July 2022. No one knows what Bakanov did (least of all Bakanov), but everyone suspected he wasn't good at it. Venedyktova spent more time chasing political rivals than rooting out corruption. Their departures produced shrugs, not shock—careers end not with bangs but unexplained boots out the door.
>
> This is the lived reality behind headlines Western experts claim to decipher. While analysts churn out war-adjacent think pieces from home offices, the real story involves institutional rot, personal ambition and leaders improvising through fog.
>
> **Community Theater Intelligence**
>
> Institutional analysis became less "intelligence community," more "community theater." Analysts warned of Russian invasion, citing Belarus's "de facto absorption"—diplomatic Anschluss straight from 1938. Yet America's top brass predicted Kyiv would fall in 72 hours. The "experts" doubled down with what some call "non-predictive intelligence floppery"—a phrase that sounds like lost Monty Python. Some cited their own previous work, creating recursive echo chambers bordering on performance art.
>
> **Democratic Discourse Failure**
>
> The real problem isn't just bureaucratic incompetence—it's fundamental failure of democratic discourse. Months before tanks rolled, voices noted the obvious: many Ukraine supporters didn't think the president was qualified, but nobody said it aloud. That mutual knowledge never became common knowledge. Rather than face reality, elite consensus doubled down: "He's not naked; you're just not looking hard enough."
>
> The expert ecosystem around Ukraine analysis was toxic from the start. The best label for these folks wasn't "analyst" but "cannon fodder"—pundits who never risked anything except being spectacularly wrong. Many suffered imposter syndrome, but uniquely, they were the only ones who didn't realize it.
>
> **Breaking Patterns**
>
> What's unique about this war is how quickly old patterns broke down. This wasn't power balancing—it was mass murder, confirmed during "slow dark blackout days in Kyiv" as Russia targeted civilian infrastructure. Putin doesn't eat because he's hungry; he eats because he's afraid. Fear has large eyes. Good luck applying conventional deterrence theories to "oligophrens and psychopaths."
>
> Meanwhile, the Western expert class keeps spinning. Beltway warbloggers and think-tankers continue circular self-citation, producing commentary less about Ukraine than maintaining their own relevance. In Ukrainian politics' "thug life" reality, their expertise looks less like insight, more like cosplay—performance for each other, not the public.
>
> **The Real Lesson**
>
> After 1252 days, the real lesson isn't about Russia, Ukraine, or the West—it's about institutions and personalities who, having failed upward, still hold the microphone.
>
> In this war, as in many others, honesty—not expertise—remains the rarest and most valuable form of accuracy. If Ukraine's brutal, improvisational politics have taught us anything, it's that reality will always outpace narratives experts try to impose. The sooner we accept that, the better chance we have to understand what's actually happening—not just in Ukraine, but anywhere stakes are this high and experts this wrong.

One thousand two hundred and thirty days ago (Day 30[1](#footnote-1)), we warned we had reached "stage five of a four-stage disaster management response." Today's [18-page Senate minority report](https://www.banking.senate.gov/imo/media/doc/final_dropping_the_baton_-_how_america_is_failing_to_use_russia_sanctions_and_export_controls_to_help_achieve_a_just_peace_in_ukraine_-_august_2025.pdf) proves that warning was prophecy, not commentary.

Trump promised to end Ukraine's war within 24 hours—53 times. By June, he'd retreated to "maybe we're better off letting them fight more." The man who campaigned on ending wars discovered that Instagram-level foreign policy doesn't deliver Instagram-speed results. He gave up without implementing a single sanction against Russia.

The administration's "appeasement strategy to end the war has only made it more deadly." Steven jets to Moscow for more talks while bodies pile up. They've innovated beyond traditional appeasement—creating a version that actively amplifies the violence it claims to stop. It's diplomatic malpractice disguised as peacemaking.

Day 30 warned of "a catastrophe of such magnitude that there is no long-term development scenario." The evidence: Russia now floats "air-truce offers" that explicitly don't end the war while Visa carves payment systems into monuments to national poets. We've transcended crisis management entirely.

This isn't foreign policy—it's performance art with a body count.

Trump's failure isn't just political embarrassment. It confirms American foreign policy has entered a post-strategic phase where promises substitute for planning and talking points replace actual policy. "The reputations of both Z and Donald are mud," we note below. When your signature promise collapses in six months, "mud" seems generous.

As Steven boards another Moscow flight and the Senate documents another leadership failure, one thing is clear: America's allies are running out of reasons to pretend otherwise.

## Day 1261
