## Day 1300 

*Date: 2025-09-15 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*Killing time *

[Image]

# Russia Invades Polish Airspace, So Trump Issues an Ultimatum to... NATO

Russian drones crossed into Polish airspace last week. NATO territory. Article 5 stuff.

Trump's response? [Lecture Europe about their energy bills](https://x.com/WhiteHouse/status/1966846399355732034).

## The Art of the Misdeal

Donald says Z and Vova hate each other too much to talk directly. So he'll play middleman. "Somehow we'll get it settled," [he said](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=F9S8VVOZXzk).

His mediation strategy? Threatening to abandon the table unless NATO does exactly what he wants. His September 13th letter reads like a ransom note written by someone who learned diplomacy on X:

> "I am ready to do major Sanctions on Russia when all NATO Nations have agreed, and started, to do the same thing, and when all NATO Nations STOP BUYING OIL FROM RUSSIA... NATO'S commitment to WIN has been far less than 100%, and the purchase of Russian Oil, by some, has been shocking!... If NATO does as I say, the WAR will end quickly... If not, you are just wasting my time, and the time, energy, and money of the United States."

This dropped three days after 19 Russian drones violated Polish territory. While NATO allies were still *processing* the escalation, Trump was busy scolding them about "shocking" energy purchases.

Meanwhile, on Saturday Secretary Rubio [parsed](https://www.state.gov/releases/office-of-the-spokesperson/2025/09/secretary-of-state-marco-rubio-remarks-to-press-2/) whether the drone incursion was "targeted" or merely "intentional"—as if there's a meaningful difference when your neighbor's military hardware is buzzing around your backyard uninvited.

## Putin's Pavlovian Response

While Trump threatens to waste everyone's "time, energy, and money," Russia has systematically ramped up strikes since he took office, according to [The New York Times](http://nyt.com).

The pattern is grimly predictable: Russia escalates immediately after diplomatic talks. It's like Putin has diplomacy-triggered Tourette's, but with missiles.

- After May's Istanbul cease-fire talks? Moscow intensified airstrikes.
- After Trump and Putin's July phone call? Russia launched 728 drones and 13 missiles less than a week later. *Thanks for the chat, Don!*
- The August Alaska summit brought temporary quiet—just long enough for Russia to stockpile drones for the deadliest Kyiv assault of the war.

Even Putin's ceasefires come with assembly required.

[Image]

Russia now produces 2,700 attack drones monthly, up from the old days of buying them from Iran like discount Ikea furniture. In July, they launched 6,300 drones against Ukraine. Last July? 426.

That's not an escalation. That's Black Friday for war crimes.

Since Trump took office, Ukrainian civilians have endured three of the deadliest months of air raids on record. But sure, let's talk about Europe's energy bills.

## The Wrong Target

So Russian drones violate NATO territory—potentially triggering Article 5—and Trump issues ultimatums to... the victims and their allies.

Not to Putin, who's running a drone assembly line like he's prepping for the apocalypse.

Not to Moscow, whose drones just treated Polish airspace like a scenic route.

But to European allies whose commitment levels apparently fall short of his exacting customer service standards.

This is dealmaking as performance art: create artificial deadlines, threaten to walk away, blame everyone except the guy literally bombing hospitals, and expect breakthrough results from theatrical tantrums.

Those 6,300 monthly Russian drones keep launching. Polish and Romanian airspace keeps getting violated.

But hey, at least NATO knows exactly where they stand with their "time-wasting" energy policies.

## Day 1301 

*Date: 2025-09-16 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*NATO-backed no-fly zone? *

[Image]

A superhero ballet dancer being crushed by a giant hand? Okay, it's a bit weird, but I suppose this sort of thing does happen from time to time. Whoever drew it neglected to put in a background, so I'll use Poland.

[Image]

Team USA still hasn't figured out whether Russia deliberately sent 20 drones into Polish airspace on June 10. Polish air defense shot down four, which you'd think might clarify intent, but apparently not for the rocket scientists at NATO who have "no news to share."

Fortunately, Secretary Rubio cleared everything up [five days later](https://www.state.gov/releases/2025/09/secretary-of-state-marco-rubio-with-gillian-turner-of-fox-news). Those Russian drones violating NATO territory? Actually proof that Trump's peace efforts are working! "This is an example of why this war needs to end," Rubio explained, deploying the kind of logic that makes Alice in Wonderland seem like a documentary. Wars escalate, you see, so Russian weapons over Polish farms are basically a peace dividend.

The Secretary was particularly helpful noting these attacks "have no impact on the front lines" - surely comforting to Polish farmers wondering if the next drone might mistake their barn for a Ukrainian power plant. But hey, "Ukrainians are conducting strikes in Russia as well," so really, who's the aggressor here?

As if to validate Rubio's brilliant analysis, Russia demonstrated its commitment to ending the war the very next day - September 16th - by launching missiles at Zaporizhia that killed one person and injured 13, including two children. Three residential buildings caught fire, requiring 78 rescuers to mop up this latest example of peace progress. Russian forces also struck 12 settlements in Kharkiv region, killing a 62-year-old man and injuring five others, because nothing says "ceasefire negotiations" like targeting elderly civilians in their homes.

Meanwhile, the same think tank analysts who've been spectacularly wrong since day one are drafting reports about "escalation dynamics" while Trump posts Truth Social ultimatums about sanctions he'll never impose. "No one in the world has done more to end the Ukraine-Russia war than President Trump," Rubio assured us, presumably keeping a straight face.

The beautiful irony is that while Rubio explains Russian attacks as peace evidence and analysts theorize about strategic ambiguity, the actual answer is a simple 540-kilometer walk from Lviv to Kyiv. Those 14-17 days would teach more about the conflict than all their diplomatic spin and footnoted reports combined.

(***Above:** Ukrzaliznytsia Oleksandr Pertsovsky explains how Team Russia has ramped up attacks on the railway infrastructure, especially on hub stations*)

You'd see the bombed apartments that expose Rubio's "both sides" nonsense. You'd meet families whose reality makes a mockery of think tank predictions. You'd walk past the infrastructure those "harmless" drones target, making the strategic picture clear to anyone not paid to obscure it.

Polish farmers don't need Rubio explaining that Russian drones over their territory indicate peaceful intentions. The grandmother whose Zaporizhia building caught fire doesn't require briefings on escalation theory. The Polish air defense crews didn't wait for State Department assessments before shooting down those four drones.

But here we are: Russian missiles hit children, NATO officials shrug, and the Secretary of State explains it's all evidence that peace talks are progressing beautifully.

The superhero ballet dancer keeps getting squeezed, but don't worry - the giant hand is providing conflict resolution services.

## Day 1302 

*Date: 2025-09-17 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

FPV drones are buzzing around Zaporizhia region. Constant shelling of Kherson. Russia bombed the logistical center of Epicenter in Kyiv region’s Fastiv district.

---

Amid all the heartbreak and destruction, these two op-eds reveal strategic confusion over Ukraine, though the authors seem blissfully unaware of it.

**Foreign Affairs** serves up the [more sophisticated confusion](https://www.foreignaffairs.com/russia/europes-delayed-reckoning-russia), correctly diagnosing Europe's predicament—caught between "a revisionist Kremlin and an indifferent White House." The brilliant solution? Europe should embrace Putin's own subversion tactics while simultaneously keeping diplomatic channels open for post-Putin reconciliation. It's the foreign policy equivalent of playing good cop and bad cop with yourself[1](#footnote-1).

**Friedman** delivers [pure therapeutic venting](https://www.nytimes.com/2025/09/16/opinion/trump-putin-russia-ukraine.html?searchResultPosition=3) disguised as analysis. His diagnosis is spot-on: "Putin keeps spitting in Trump's eye and Trump keeps telling the world that it's raining." But Friedman's remedy is essentially screaming "DO SOMETHING!" at his television. His Chamberlain comparison would make high school debate coaches wince[2](#footnote-2).

**The deeper delusion** both share is their romantic amnesia about pre-war Ukraine: a sham democracy, a primitive post-Soviet feudal autocracy that functioned not through elections or institutions, but through periodic popular uprisings (2004, 2014). Without those revolts, Ukraine would not have existed to be invaded.

The cardboard kids come to mind[3](#footnote-3).

This matters because both authors blithely assume victory leads somewhere obvious. Foreign Affairs envisions Putin's Russia eventually rejoining the "collaborative international frameworks"—presumably after some therapeutic soul-searching. Friedman wants Ukrainian EU membership to solve everything with Brussels bureaucracy.

But what happens after we *win* the war is anyone's guess. Should Ukraine embrace pan-European socialist remedies? Emulate Ireland's Silicon Valley transformation? Neither author bothers asking, content to debate tactics while ignoring the endgame.

## Day 1303 

*Date: 2025-09-18 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*The memory police *

[Image]

Another triumphant day for Putin's "special military operation," now in its delightful fourth year! Let's summarize yesterday’s achievements:

**The Pokrovsk Masterclass:** Russian forces demonstrated their tactical brilliance by attempting 43 separate attacks, managing to lose 160 soldiers (97 permanently off the payroll) while capturing exactly zero Ukrainian positions. They also generously donated 5 cars and 15 drones to the Ukrainian recycling program. Three fights are still ongoing because apparently some people are slow learners.

**National Statistics Update:** The running tally now shows Russia has kindly provided Ukraine with approximately 1,098,380 fewer personnel to worry about, plus a museum-worthy collection of 11,191 tanks and 32,879 artillery systems. Today's contribution included 2 more tanks and 33 artillery pieces, because sharing is caring.

**The Drone Safari:** In a stunning display of precision targeting, Russian UAVs managed to set fire to... warehouses and private houses. In Kyiv region, they successfully attacked civilian infrastructure (shocking, I know), while in Zaporizhia they launched 551 strikes on 18 settlements, apparently using the "spray and pray" methodology taught at the finest Russian military academies.

**Infrastructure Specialists:** Not to be outdone, Russian forces in Dnipropetrovsk region demonstrated their engineering expertise by damaging a gas pipeline—because nothing says "liberation" like cutting off heating in winter.

**The Kherson Hat Trick:** Seven injured civilians, including a child, because targeting kindergartens requires the same skill set as military objectives, obviously. They also helpfully redecorated 35 private houses and some garages, free of charge.

---

Yesterday, I listened to Lukashenko opine on historiography.

> "We understand that Belarus, a third of a century into its sovereign history, is still very young. The history of a sovereign state is only just beginning," he said. ([Belta](https://belta.by/president/view/lukashenko-orientiruet-na-edinuju-strategiju-formirovanija-istoricheskoj-pamjati-737783-2025/). September 17, 2025)

The Belarusian dictator said historians analyze documents and facts, constructing a chronology of events. The ideological task, he emphasized, is to transform this material into narratives, arguments and counterarguments, and work with them in the information field. He used the unconstitutional referendum in early 1995 as an example.

> "Here, scholars, educators, journalists, and experts must act within the framework of a unified strategy for the formation of historical memory," he said.

[Image]

That was 30 years ago, when Viktor Sheiman was the hatchetman of the make-Belarus-a-dictatorship project. Kash reminded me of Viktor.

What Lukashenko articulated wasn't novel—it was a playbook. The same playbook that began in Minsk in 1995, moved to Moscow in 2000, and now defines The Golden Age of Ameriʞa.

The pattern: seize the historical narrative first, then create a "unified strategy for the formation of historical memory." Politicians, scholars, educators, journalists, photographers, comedians, cartoonists and experts fall in line—or get replaced.

Lukashenko's casual reference to transforming historical material into "narratives, arguments and counterarguments" for deployment "in the information field" reveals how authoritarians view truth: as raw material to be processed, shaped, weaponized.

This is what I meant when I wrote that under illiberalism, "the future is the past and where cranes fear to tread, the bulldozer will boldly go." The bulldozer doesn't just destroy buildings—it levels the landscape of memory itself[1](#footnote-1).

The unconstitutional referendum wasn't just politics—it was a laboratory experiment in rewriting reality in real time. Thirty years later, he's still perfecting the formula.

## The Sophistication Problem

Those shocked by rising demagoguery should remember we're dealing with leaders "vaccinated" by social media algorithms against progress. Why use force when you can rewrite what happened? Why break bodies when you can break the collective capacity to distinguish truth from fiction?

The most chilling aspect is the matter-of-fact assumption that of course historians should serve power, of course memory should be managed, of course truth should be strategic.

After thirty years of practice, it's no longer ideology—it's just technique.

When the population internalizes the habit of accepting manufactured memory as authentic experience, the work is done. The most unpleasant consequence of patrimonialism is exactly what Lukashenko described: institutionalized historical revisionism.

[Image]

The Kash-Viktor connection isn't casual. Both represent the operational hatchetmen of authoritarianism—people who transform crude power instincts into systematic methodology.

We're moving toward a new normal, but not the one we hoped for. The arrow of historical time is accelerating, but acceleration just means reaching the destination faster. And the destination, if current trends hold, is a world where confidence about controlling historical memory becomes universal standard.

The template spreads. The memory police are hiring.

## Day 1304 

*Date: 2025-09-19 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*Poem of the week *

[Image]

Убей президента!

Убей эту сволочь, которая

так гнусно топорщит усы

над нашими всё же просторами,

над ликом родимой красы.

Чем можешь убей, не раздумывай,

возьми автомат иль топор

и вскрой эту голову “умную”,

и выбрось в клоаку, как сор.

Убей эту мразь авторучкою

иль зонтиком, или “пером”,

иль вовсе ма—аленькой штучкою,

подброшенной в терпкий ром.

Убей же врага, чтобы молодость

твоя не осталась в дерьме,

чтоб Край наш

воспрянул с гордостью

во славе, а не в ярме!

Убей аккуратно, дерзостно,

как может убить лишь Бог,

покончи геройски с мерзостью

и выкинь её за порог!

Очищенный дом беларуский наш,

проветренный,

солнцу представленный,

он будет закрыт для усатых саш,

но миру свободному явленный.

*November 30, 1995 ([Славамір Адамовіч](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavamir_Adamovich))*

Those who lie because of their own ideological persuasion are no longer journalists, they are propagandists. They should be removed, or acknowledged as such.
>
> 6. I have found foreign perceptions of Trump to be heavily unbalanced and at times completely out of step with the feelings of average Americans. This is because their beliefs are driven by media that skews left of center.
>
> 7. Ukraine is many wonderful things, but it is undergoing its own difficulties with free speech. One, in particular, is self-censorship. Journalists have acknowledged withholding critical reporting on government missteps to preserve morale or to avoid exposing corruption that might cause foreign partners to hesitate on approving aid. Opposition media has also been subjected to government pressure.
>
> 8. There has also been no time in Ukrainian history where the Presidential office has held and concentrated so much power. And democracy doesn’t mean a lack of discord or strife. America’s system was built to be resilient because of the belief in her people. A belief I hold. Nations fall, empires collapse, tyrants rise and fade, yet the idea of self-government persists. This is because it speaks to something eternal in the human spirit: the longing to be free.

Ah, how perfectly martial: a West Point graduate lecturing us on the delicate balance of democracy while apparently confusing a television studio with a battlefield. One might think that four years at the Academy would have taught Meaghan the difference between enemy propaganda and Jimmy Kimmel making jokes, but evidently the curriculum has gaps.

And what delicious timing: to return from the actual trenches of an actual war against actual tyranny, only to declare that America's greatest threat to liberty is... late-night television accountability. One imagines the Ukrainian journalists—dodging actual missiles while our author tends to actual wounds—might have a rather different perspective on what constitutes press suppression.

But then again, when your training teaches you that complex problems require decisive action and clear chain of command, perhaps it's only natural to see messy democratic discourse as something that needs a good, crisp correction. How wonderfully American: exporting freedom abroad while demanding discipline at home.

## Day 1306

*Date: 2025-09-22 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*Putin's four-dimensional chess*

[Image]

So here's the story according to Bloomberg's unnamed "[people close to the Kremlin](https://www.bloomberg.com/news/articles/2025-09-20/putin-decides-russia-can-step-up-ukraine-attacks-and-trump-won-t-act)" (who are definitely real and not just Putin's press secretary with a fake mustache): Vlad met Trump in Alaska, decided the orange man won't lift a finger for Ukraine, and is now bombing the shit out of everything because *strategy*.

Meanwhile, the Institute for the Study of War—those same brilliant minds who once declared Putin a "rational actor"—has helpfully explained that this Bloomberg leak is actually Putin's 4D chess move to... wait for it... *[psychologically manipulate Western audiences through strategic media placement](https://understandingwar.org/research/russia-ukraine/russian-offensive-campaign-assessment-september-20-2025/)*.

Let's pause here. We're supposed to believe Putin—a man who invaded his neighbor because he was afraid of NATO expansion—is now playing subtle information warfare games through financial news outlets? The same Putin whose propaganda apparatus includes gems like "Ukrainian Nazi biothrowing mosquitoes"?

ISW breathlessly informs us that Putin "may have allowed these Kremlin sources" to leak, because apparently the FSB now runs on an honor system where officials casually chat with Bloomberg reporters. They've cracked the code: it's all about "exploiting friction between European and US officials."

Both reports share the delightful assumption that Putin's escalation represents some sort of calculated master plan rather than, say, the flailing of an autocrat whose "special military operation" has turned into a three-year meat grinder. Bloomberg frames it as Putin forcing Ukraine into talks "on his terms"—which is odd, considering his terms seem to involve Ukraine not existing.

We called this out back on Day 150ish when we noted how ISW had mistakenly assessed Putin as a "rational actor" in December 2021. Apparently some lessons take longer to learn than others[1](#footnote-1).

The real comedy gold? ISW warning that this Bloomberg article might "create fear in Ukrainian society ahead of Winter 2025-2026." Because apparently Ukrainians, who've been getting bombed for three years straight, were just waiting for a Bloomberg scoop to realize things might get cold and explodey.

Here's a radical thought: maybe Putin is bombing Ukraine's infrastructure not because of some elaborate post-Alaska revelation, but because bombing civilian infrastructure is what war criminals do. Maybe the timing has less to do with Trump's Alaska body language and more to do with Russia's desperate need to show *something* resembling progress before another winter of international isolation.

But sure, let's pretend this is all part of Putin's brilliant psychological operation rather than the predictable tantrum of a dictator whose invasion went sideways. After all, nothing says "strategic genius" like being surprised that invading your neighbor might take longer, er, than 1,306 days.

Watching these unfolding events, aspiring philosopher Volodymyr Pastukhov [is starting to think](https://t.me/v_pastukhov/1659) that every country's political trajectory needs its own "Kirov assassination moment." We still don't know for certain why Nikolaev killed Kirov in 1934, or whether he even did it. Was it an impulsive act by a cuckolded husband, or was the infidelity merely cover for deeper political motives? Perhaps it was simply years of accumulated rage against perceived injustice. The specifics, ultimately, don't matter.

What matters is that Stalin seized this assassination to crush all opposition and cement his personal dictatorship. The parallels write themselves: the precise motivations behind political violence become irrelevant once power decides to exploit the moment. History suggests we should be less concerned with parsing Putin's Alaska revelations and more focused on watching who benefits from the narrative that emerges.

[1](#footnote-anchor-1)

[Day 150ish](https://abdymok.substack.com/p/day-150ish?utm_source=publication-search). *Spontaneous mass confusion* (July 22, 2022)

## Day 1307 

*Date: 2025-09-23 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

[Image]

In mid-September from Kyiv, you'll find both Orion the Hunter and Sirius in the eastern part of the sky before dawn. Orion’s Belt points southeastward.

[Image]

Best seen during the waxing crescent moon phase.

---

On the war jacket, Russia dropped tonnes more TNT on Zaporizhia again last night.

[Image]

Russian forces are now within tube artillery range of Zaporizhia city (population 700,000). They have been pounding the metropolis and environs with "Correctable Aerial Bombs” (KABs) for the past couple of weeks.

The Telegram channel [Para Pax](https://t.me/parapax/4190) posted this yesterday.

I don’t know where they got the [video](https://t.me/parapax/4190), which illustrates what we’re up against.

---

Z arrived in NYC yesterday for the UNGA.

He and Donald are scheduled to chat today, as are their spouses. Wonderful.

---

A "democratic autocrat" is about as much of an oxymoron as a "scientific feeling." But you can't choose your contradictions—an artifact remains an artifact, and it now needs explaining.

Jamie does a horrible job[1](#footnote-1).

Personally, the situation most closely resembles those "party purges" that early Bolsheviks practiced religiously. A movement that seizes power inevitably attracts hordes of "fellow travelers" who see it primarily as career advancement opportunities.

The essence doesn't change even if movements like Z's were originally conceived for genuine transformation. First, that was ages ago!—now everything's deadly serious, subordinated to lining pockets and preserving power. Second, initially, these opportunities were meant "for the people."

In Ukraine, the ruling party doesn't need "democratic suckers"—it will fleece them, retaining only distilled loyal cores that oscillate with the line instinctively.

Meanwhile, Putin performs his own purge theater. His defense industry can't afford new factories while he waves nuclear treaties around like party invitations. The man who's violated every international agreement since Yalta now lectures about arms control—a real "circus with bears.[2](#footnote-2)"

And Trump? He's discovered autism's cause: Tylenol. "This is based on what I feel," the president declared, dismissing decades of research for his gut instincts. Don't take Tylenol while pregnant, he commanded. Space out vaccines. The hepatitis B shot for infants? Disgraceful. His health team—Kennedy, Makary, Bhattacharya, Oz—stood awkwardly behind him as he went rogue, endorsing fringe theories they wouldn't touch. "They are waiting for certain studies. I don't—I just want to say it like it is.[3](#footnote-3)"

The pattern's identical across continents: paranoid leaders have reached the same conclusion. In besieged fortresses, internal criticism becomes externally weaponized. Ukrainian lawmakers questioning corruption become Russian stooges. Russian oligarchs become Western agents. American scientists become deep-state conspirators.

Ukraine fights for European values while adopting Russian methods. Russia claims superpower status while its economy resembles a Ponzi scheme. America preaches evidence-based policy while its president prescribes medical advice based on "feelings."

Here's where [Aumann's theorem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aumann%27s_agreement_theorem#:~:text=The%20theorem%20was%20proved%20by,theoretic%20definition%20of%20common%20knowledge.) becomes darkly relevant: rational actors with shared priors who make their posteriors common knowledge cannot disagree. Yet Putin, Z, and Trump—all facing similar evidence about democratic governance, expertise, and legitimacy—have reached identical conclusions through supposedly different reasoning. Their convergence on authoritarian solutions suggests either they're sharing priors we can't see, or they've stopped being rational actors altogether.

The drones still fly. The purges accelerate. And somewhere in Odesa, people wait out the latest air alert in a bomb shelter decorated by the Feldman sisters.

[Image]

## Day 1308 

*Date: 2025-09-24 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*The emperor's new analgesic*

[Image]

*Those lips, that brain, this delusion[1](#footnote-1)*

The difference between everyone knowing something and everyone knowing that everyone else knows it sounds like academic hairsplitting until you realize it’s the difference between whispered corridors and newspaper headlines. Between maintaining a fiction and admitting a truth.

Take this proposition: *Ukraine’s wartime leadership has become increasingly authoritarian while its Western allies either don’t notice or don’t care.*

Until recently, this was merely mutual knowledge—diplomats discussed it in secure rooms, journalists alluded to it carefully, Ukrainian MPs grumbled privately. Everyone knew it, but nobody was sure that everyone else knew that they knew it.

Then came July’s cardboard revolution. A small protest near Z’s compound with handwritten signs: “You aren’t a tsar” and “If you stole less, I’d bury my friends less.” Like the child in Andersen’s tale, these protesters pointed and said what everyone could see: the democratic clothes weren’t there.

The moment was transformative not because of what it revealed—everyone already knew about the power consolidation, attacks on anti-corruption agencies, increasingly personalized rule—but because it made this knowledge *common*. Suddenly, everyone knew that everyone else had seen the signs.

What’s deliciously ironic is how Trump’s about-face on Ukraine promises to make things even worse. As America signals its willingness to sell Ukraine a bunch of weapons, the incentives for maintaining even democratic pretense are evaporating. Why worry about oversight when Washington values “getting things done”? Why fret about press freedom when your most important ally campaigns against “fake news”?

[Image]

The common knowledge problem works in reverse too. Just as everyone knowing that everyone knows about democratic backsliding can catalyze change, everyone knowing that everyone knows that *nobody really cares* can catalyze acceleration. When European diplomats express private concerns while maintaining public support, when Western media writes careful analyses while avoiding condemnation, when Ukrainian MPs grumble quietly while voting in line—this creates common knowledge of complicity.

Trump offers Z something invaluable: a role model who successfully transitioned from con man to strongman while keeping key constituencies happy. The new transactional US-Ukraine relationship means results matter more than process, outcomes more than institutions. Why maintain the exhausting fiction of sharing liberal values when autocratic efficiency gets better reviews from your most important audience?

Z’s new clothes are becoming invisible just when visibility matters least.

In Andersen’s fairy tale, the child’s observation breaks the spell because everyone suddenly realizes they’ve been participating in collective self-deception. But what happens when the revelation comes just as the kingdom decides that naked emperors are actually rather fashionable? What happens when common knowledge of democratic decay coincides with common knowledge that such decay is the price of doing business?

The answer is that the clothes disappear entirely. And everyone pretends not to notice, because noticing has become inconvenient to the new consensus. The child pointing at the naked emperor isn’t brave anymore—they’re just rude.

[Video: https://youtu.be/c1tjh\_ZO\_tY]

*Mutual knowledge becomes common knowledge through simultaneous revelation. But sometimes, common knowledge of uncomfortable truths is less powerful than common knowledge of comfortable lies.*

[1](#footnote-anchor-1)

[Z’s new clothes](https://abdymok.substack.com/p/Zs-new-clothes) (November 30, 2021)

I had intended to write a long uninspiring post about the defense of Kupyansk in Kharkiv region.

[Image]

Specifically about the enemy bridgehead on the western bank of the Oskil, stretching for almost 40 kilometers, varying between 3-5 kilometers wide, because its dismemberment is near and dear to my heart.

[Image]

But I’ve been distracted by pundits bloviating about what politicians have been saying at the UNGA.

Trump was **locked in** all day yesterday. Now we’ll spend the next month dragging him back to earth. Let’s start with Ukraine.

He didn’t actually say anything new—though try explaining that to people who treat his words like gospel at a tent revival. The key is metaphor. Luckily, late-night comics have it covered. Jimmy Kimmel nailed it: Trump changes positions so fast he should be sponsored by IKEA—”Some assembly required, instructions in Swedish, and it still collapses.”

That’s where we are. Z and Lavrov both show up, each promising victory. The EU waves €50 billion around. Trump does what any seasoned con artist would: nods at everyone, pockets the cash, and plays the waiting game. Eventually someone blinks, someone else gets the call, and Umerov buys another house in Boca Raton.

Anne [gushes](https://archive.fo/XoqsB#selection-615.0-922.0) over Ukraine’s manicure-to-missile pivot—nail techs building drones that torch Russian oil. Nick dutifully [tracks](https://edition.cnn.com/2025/09/24/europe/trump-ukraine-putin-land-analysis-intl) Trump’s flip-flops like they’re foreign policy instead of performance art. David gravely [explains](https://www.nytimes.com/2025/09/24/us/politics/trump-ukraine.html) how the man ping-pongs between “Ukraine’s doomed” and “Ukraine’s unstoppable” with all the authority of a Magic 8-Ball.

The Kremlin plays animal kingdom—Russia’s a paper tiger, then a bear, but never a paper bear. Z sticks to his greatest hits: friends and weapons, because treaties don’t stop Kalibrs and his English-language vocab is limited.

Meanwhile, the pundit class nods sagely, quotes extensively, and pats itself on the back for moral clarity—all while eyeing gas prices.

Which brings us back to Kupyansk and “the large air compressor with a variety of accessories.” It’s for boring (#2 on the list).

This gadget provides sustained high-pressure, high-volume air delivery that can power multiple heavy-duty tools continuously—the perfect donation to defenders of Kupyansk, who can use it for numerous heavy-duty tasks, such as tunnel excavation and surface mining operations.

Read Zaluzhny’s latest article, titled “Innovation as Core of Strategic Resilience: Denying Russia the Power to Dictate Terms Through War,” [here](https://zn.ua/eng/innovation-as-core-of-strategic-resilience-denying-russia-the-power-to-dictate-terms-through-war.html).

Pete obvi is preparing for wars that can no longer be fought while Zaluzhny maps out a reality where “soldiers found themselves trapped under the ‘lower sky’ of constant drone surveillance and attacks” and “the battlefield became completely transparent, manoeuvre all but impossible.”

DoW is still thinking in terms of massive formations and traditional combined arms while the actual war has evolved into something where “defence is organised as dispersed positions held by small groups operating autonomously under extreme strain” and “the very idea of a secure rear is fading, since its customary location behind the forward echelons—anywhere within 40 kilometres—is no longer tenable.”

The disaster isn’t just tactical—it’s conceptual. American military planners are preparing for the wrong war entirely.

---

On the USA jacket, [Soros](https://www.nytimes.com/2025/09/25/us/politics/justice-trump-george-soros-foundation.html).

[Image]

Here’s the thing about getting expelled from Belarus for “interfering in internal affairs.” It was 1997, and we had been running the third sector like liberalism-peddling maniacs since ‘94, helping build things—legal libraries, high school debate teams, independent media, history books, internet connectivity, etc. Real money for real people doing real work that made the country’s potato-shaped dictator sweat through his polyester suit.

The photo tells you everything: me flanked by the chairman of the Supreme Economic Court, a couple of state security goons who looked like they’d flunked out of villain school, the foreign minister, and US Ambassador Kenneth Yalowitz. Classic post-Soviet kabuki—everyone pretending this was diplomacy while the secret police took notes for my deportation file.

> Free money,  
> when it travels at a certain trajectory and speed,  
> can turn anyone into a target.

Yesterday the Trump Justice Department announced they’re going after Soros. The irony practically writes itself in crayon. Same playbook Lukashenko used: foreign interference, destabilizing activities, funding opposition. Except now it’s coming from Washington, not some third-world capital with pretensions. Pam, Kash, and that prick Aakash have stumbled ass-backwards into adopting the paranoid fantasies of every tin-pot dictator who couldn’t handle a few lawyers learning how to read contracts.

The network’s transition to US politics, pencils and oranges—safe, neutered, committee-approved programming—coincided with my departure. I looked at their website recently and nearly fell asleep scrolling through the 2000s. The conspicuous absence of 1995 from [the timeline](https://www.opensocietyfoundations.org/who-we-are/our-history) is particularly rich, since that year was peak disruption, when informed citizens who knew their rights weren’t afraid to use them.

What’s darkly hilarious is watching American officials discover the same paranoia that made Lukashenko shit his pants. They’re not just targeting Soros—they’re taking a blowtorch to America’s own liberalism promotion apparatus while genuflecting to every strongman who ever whined about criticism. The mouth-breathing security operatives in that ‘96 photo knew exactly what they were looking at: a threat to their pathetic little fiefdom.

The real tragedy? Team Trump has reached the same moronic conclusion.

## Day 1315

*Date: 2025-09-30 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*Unification day *

[Image]

Three years ago today, Russia declared its annexation of four Ukrainian regions: Donetsk, Luhansk, Kherson and Zaporizhia, roughly the size of the state of Virginia. If we add Crimea, which Russia annexed in 2014, it’s the size of Alabama, a little more than 52,000 square miles. The Kremlin has published Putin’s congratulatory address on the anniversary of the *reunification*.

[Image]

The 47th President promised he’d end the war “in 24 hours” 53 times on the campaign trail. Instead, after fawning over Putin with a red-carpet welcome in Alaska this summer, Trump has delivered exactly what his critics predicted: zero progress, maximum spectacle, and a war now in its 12th year.

---

Yesterday’s lengthy interview with Kherson Mayor Volodymyr Mykolaienko, who was released after three years of torture in a Russian prison this summer, details how quickly the Rusians seized Kherson in February 2022[1](#footnote-1).

> It was very painful to watch how in a few hours they were already standing near Kherson, on the left bank. There was confusion. How can this be? After all, only weeks earlier [[February 12, 2022](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=HEybCJtW7Gc)], Z visited, attended exercises [tactical and special exercises organized by the Ministry of Internal Affairs]. They told him everything was fine. The president even held a press conference and said officials were not worried [about a Russia invasion], but we all saw what happened next…

[Image]

Remember when Z confidently assured Ukrainians that “the Russian people will not go to war against the Ukrainian people”? That was February 19, 2022—just five days before the invasion. Tragically, many Ukrainians in southeastern parts of the country believed him and didn’t evacuate.

This catastrophic intelligence failure—or willful denial—cost tens of thousands of Ukrainian lives in Mariupol alone, not to mention the tens of thousands of Ukrainian children kidnapped and deported to Russia. Cities that could have been evacuated became death traps. Families that might have fled stayed put, trusting their president’s assurances right up until Russian tanks rolled in.

It remains one of the most devastating miscalculations of the war, and Z has never adequately answered for it.

## Day 1316

*Date: 2025-10-01 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*Animals*

```
Have you forgotten what we were like then
when we were still first rate
and the day came fat with an apple in its mouth

it’s no use worrying about Time
but we did have a few tricks up our sleeves
and turned some sharp corners

the whole pasture looked like our meal
we didn’t need speedometers
we could manage cocktails out of ice and water

I wouldn’t want to be faster
or greener than now if you were with me O you
were the best of all my days
```

[1950[1](#footnote-1)]

Team USA’s mistake was thinking it could woo Russia away from China. Just ask Elbridge Colby, undersecretary of war, who can’t bring himself to say that Russia invaded Ukraine. He argues unconvincingly this is not only practical but essential strategy. Spoiler: it isn’t. China’s big mistake was thinking it could use Russia to further its own interests at the expense of western civilization everywhere. Putin made two catastrophic errors: thinking he could walk over Ukraine in 2022, and deciding that Russia becoming a vassal again—1,000 years later—of its eastern neighbor was safer than playing by civilized rules

[Image]

As for less-developed nations—the so-called global south—they don’t get a vote, because, collectively, they account for less than 20% of the world’s GDP (once you strip out China) and we’re still bailing some of them out, e.g. Argentina.

Let’s unpack this spectacular failure of strategic thinking.

Washington spent eight months trying to invent a wedge between Moscow and Beijing. The logic seemed sound: exploit historical tensions, offer economic carrots, remind Putin that Russia is a European power. Colby and others sold this vision hard—détente with Moscow, focus on real threat in Beijing. But this fundamentally misread Putin’s calculus. He isn’t shopping for the best deal—he is shopping for survival.

And here’s the kicker: even if the strategy had been sound, Team USA has become too *unwoke* to execute it. The bozos in charge can’t even keep the government running. Hard to run sophisticated great power diplomacy when you are fomenting civil war at home and deploying soldiers to large American cities.

Beijing made its own blunder. Xi thought he could harness Russian resentment as a battering ram while keeping Moscow on a short leash. Instead, Russia’s Ukraine invasion—which was supposed to last days, not years—created a junior partner so economically needy and diplomatically isolated that it became a liability. China got a dependent, not a weapon.

And Russia? Putin looked at his options after the Ukraine debacle and chose subordination. The irony is brutal: a country that spent centuries expanding eastward, that still nurses historical grievances about Mongol domination, willingly walked into economic and strategic dependence on China.

Here’s the darkest part: Russia’s elite couldn’t care less that the country’s population will essentially become a large slave labor force used by China to extract natural resources. As long as the oligarchs and siloviki keep their dachas and offshore accounts, what’s a generation or three of resource colonization?

Russia now sells oil at a discount to China, buys Chinese goods at a premium, and watches Chinese influence creep into Central Asia. Moscow calls it a “strategic partnership.” Beijing calls it whatever makes Putin feel better. Team USA calls it a problem—when it can spare attention from MAGA chaos.

Sometimes everyone loses. But Russia’s people will lose longest.

---

### The Algorithm Ate My Lunch

I wanted everyone to feel the way my teeth vibrated, but when I posted my glow-in-the-dark selfie only the closet mermaids noticed. I didn’t blame anyone. I knew the oceans were walking heavy-footed on the land. It was one of those afternoons when I was sure that God was the series of tubes connecting my brain to the torso of a deer. Was it possible that the self was larger than the country that birthed it? Could a mouth open large enough to accommodate the girth of a bloated nation’s whale? I hung a sign that read “Hope” on the taxidermied body of an owl and waited for applause. Children gathered around to worship, sticking microchips and slivers of sunlight in its missing, blinking eye. — [Joanna Fuhrman](https://poetrysociety.org/poems-essays/in-their-own-words/joanna-fuhrman-on-the-algorithm-ate-my-lunch)

---

Computational social scientist Petter Tornberg [built](https://www.businessinsider.com/ai-chatbots-less-toxic-social-networks-twitter-simulation-2023-11) a fake social media platform, populated it with 500 LLMs playing ordinary Americans, and watched them recreate every pathology we’ve spent twenty years complaining about—without any algorithmic nudging required.

Starting from Thomas Schelling’s 1969 checkerboard model showing how mild preferences for similar neighbors produce total segregation, Tornberg extended the logic to online communities. Turns out digital spaces segregate even faster than physical ones. The integrated state is just unstable. One person leaves, the neighborhood tips, cascade complete.

**People act against their own stated preferences due to problems of self-control and commitment**.

Then came the main event: LLMs with backstories from actual voter surveys, turned loose on a bare-bones platform where they could post, repost, and follow each other. No engagement algorithms. No trigger bubbles. Just agents interacting.

Results? Echo chambers formed immediately—Democrats and Republicans stopped following each other. Attention distributed via power law—a few users dominated while most got ignored. The social media prism emerged—extreme voices accumulated the most followers. Everything we hate about TikTok, generated organically from basic human (or human-simulating) behavior.

Tornberg tried fixes: chronological timelines, hiding political affiliations, showing constructive posts first, even showing the *least* liked posts first. Nothing worked. Some interventions made things worse. The structural outcomes proved robust to perturbation, like the Schelling effect on steroids.

The kicker: platforms aren’t just reflecting our preferences, they’re reshaping our politics by defining who gets attention. Tornberg’s separate study of politicians across countries found **radical right populists specifically leveraging misinformation as strategy**, that is, conspiricism, intertwined with social media’s attention economy. We recall that The New York Times started writing clickbait headlines on Bill Keller’s watch.

So no, you can’t just log off. The incentive structures are leaking into everything—media, politics, culture. We moved from Fordist machine epistemology (society as designed system) to complexity epistemology (**society as self-organizing swarm**), and discovered that emergent outcomes still embed power structures. They’re just harder to see.

Tornberg’s nostalgic for ‘90s internet—ICQ’s “talk to random strangers” button, genuine connection, pre-commercialized chaos. **Maybe we need fundamental redesign**, not cosmetic algorithm tweaks. But nobody’s quite figured out how to build platforms that don’t automatically generate exactly the problems we’re trying to escape.

## Day 1318ish

*Date: 2025-10-03 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*Where were we? *

[Image]

I was time travelling in still frames.

Meanwhile, someone was nice enough to put me back on YouTube’s family premium plan, enabling me to [listen](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=IN5lTJtkZrA), ad-free, to Kiril Rogov talk about his essay, titled “Military Dead End” ([Военный Тупик](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=IN5lTJtkZrA))[1](#footnote-1).

Here’s the nut graph:

> A military **stalemate** is a new framework that will shape how both Russia and the international community understand the current stage of the conflict. It will strip away arguments from those opposed to military aid to Ukraine, increase discipline within the sanctions coalition, change the agenda of potential negotiations on freezing the conflict, and force Putin to face a difficult choice: a deep financial crisis by the end of next year or abandoning another attempt to seize northern Donbas.

Kirill traces how understanding of the war has evolved through several phases. Initially, Russian military superiority seemed absolute. By summer 2022, it became clear Russia lacked a war-ready army. Ukraine’s autumn counteroffensive suggested Western support could tip the balance. However, Ukraine’s failed 2023 offensive showed Russia had stabilized its military operations, leading to the “war of attrition” framework that dominated thinking for two years.

This framework assumed Russia would eventually prevail due to superior manpower and economic resources. However, several factors prevented this outcome: Putin’s reluctance to conduct broad mobilization, drone technology neutralizing numerical advantages, and continued Western support for Ukraine. As former Ukrainian Commander-in-Chief Valeriy Zaluzhny argued in 2023, technological capabilities have reached a stage—similar to World War I—where offensive breakthroughs are impossible without new technological advances[2](#footnote-2).

So, stalemate.

Next up, Anne, who in an interview with the [Evropeiska Pravda](https://www.eurointegration.com.ua/eng/interview/2025/10/2/7221502/) ezine doesn’t directly address Russia’s military capabilities, but her comments about Europe needing to take ownership of its defense suggest she also sees Russia as a manageable threat if properly confronted[3](#footnote-3).

While Kirill credits continued Western (especially European) support as crucial to preventing Ukraine’s defeat in the “war of attrition,” Anne explicitly challenges the premise that “the US is the key to Ukraine’s security,” noting that European financial and military contributions actually exceed American aid when properly calculated. She, like [Timothy Snyder](https://yes-ukraine.org/en/photo-and-video/video/shchorichna-zustrich-yaltinskoyi-yevropeyskoyi-strategiyi-yes-yak-zavershiti-viynu/shcho-istoriki-vvazhatimut-ukrayinskoyu-peremogoyu), argues that waiting for Team USA to solve the problem is dangerous.

According to The [Economist](https://www.economist.com/briefing/2025/10/02/why-russias-micro-aggressions-against-europe-are-proliferating), Russia’s is deploying grey-zone tactics precisely *because* conventional warfare has stalled[4](#footnote-4). The drone incursions, airspace violations, and sabotage operations across Europe aren’t signs of strength—they’re strategic pivots born from battlefield failure. As Romania’s defense minister notes: “They cannot make even one step ahead in Ukraine.”

Russia’s grey-zone escalation actually validates both Kirill’s **stalemate** thesis and Applebaum’s argument. If Russia truly had conventional military superiority, it wouldn’t need to fly drones over Danish oilfields or hack Norwegian dams. These are tactics of weakness masquerading as intimidation.

Anduril Industries’ Barracuda missile family can end this mess in a couple of months.

Tomahawk missiles to Ukraine would “destroy” U.S.-Russia relations, he warns—apparently bombing Lviv, flying drones over Danish oilfields, and hacking Norwegian dams are all fine for “positive trends” in bilateral bonhomie, but giving Kyiv 1,550-mile-range cruise missiles crosses some imaginary red line only visible from the Kremlin.

[Image]

The timing? *Chef’s kiss.* Less than two months after Trump’s Alaska summit, where presumably everyone shook hands and pretended this wasn’t a shitshow, Putin is reduced to flying fibre optic drones into Kramatorsk and blowing up random sedans. If Russia truly had any conventional superiority, he’d be advancing. Instead, war crimes.

As we pointed out on Friday, this *is* a military dead end[1](#footnote-1). Attrition assumes grinding down works. History says it doesn’t.

Stalemate is attrition’s confession of failure.

Russia can’t make even one step ahead in Ukraine, so Moscow pivots to grey-zone theater: sabotage, airspace violations, the whole repertoire of weakness masquerading as intimidation. Cheap drones have frozen the battlefield into deadlock. Russia’s flying 34,000 drones into Ukraine this year—nine times more than last—but it’s desperation math, not dominance.

[The myth that only American aid matters](https://www.kielinstitut.de/topics/war-against-ukraine/ukraine-support-tracker/). European contributions exceed US support. Russia’s manageable *if confronted*. But first, apparently, Team USA needs to negotiate whether giving Ukraine the ability to shoot back at the country bombing its cities is compatible with Putin’s feelings.

*Date: 2025-10-08 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*Turning off the lights*

The current population of Chernihiv region, roughly the size of the US state of New Jersey, is approximately 1 million people. Team Russia is trying very hard to turn off their lights. Drone and missile attacks every night. [Nyzhyn](https://t.me/chernigivoblenergo/4148) was smashed last night, [Pryluky](https://newsukraine.rbc.ua/news/russian-drone-strike-hits-energy-facility-1759827046.html) the night before.

---

Let me see if I can find the article…

…Took me less than 10 seconds: Magnitogorsk Journal; Russians Take a Stroll Down Easy Street, USA[1](#footnote-1). The author later became executive editor of [The New York Times](http://nyt.com), but not because of his reporting about the collapse of the USSR.

Bill came to mind because he misquoted me in the above article. I am from Millwood—not Norwood—New York and only nominally.

I just skimmed through an essay by another former NYT flak about our bloody mess in Ukraine.

[Image]

We pointed out laboriously (for me) last week that that a military stalemate—**not a war of attrition**—now represents the framework for understanding the current stage of the Russia-Ukraine conflict[2](#footnote-2). Robert’s observations from the zero line creates a significant tension with our illuminating observation: *stalemate is attrition’s confession of failure*.

Robert describes a war that Ukraine is approaching as potentially winnable through attrition management. Yet this very claim contains its own refutation. When combatants stop talking about *winning* through attrition and start talking about *surviving* it, they have already admitted the strategy has failed. The essay is full of this language of mere survival: evacuation delays, recruitment collapse, rotation as “a way of conserving manpower,” command posts designed “so that they could be evacuated almost instantly.” These are not the practices of a side pursuing attrition victory. These are the practices of a side trying not to disappear entirely.

The stalemate, in other words, is not a failure of strategy to be overcome. It is the *political outcome* toward which exhaustion tends. Stalemate is not something that replaces attrition; it is what attrition *becomes* when attrition fails. Ingenuity in defense of a stalemate is not the same as ingenuity in pursuit of victory.

## Day 1324 

*Date: 2025-10-09 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*Maps over cheerful pedagogy*

Walking past a placard reading “**WRATH: OUR TRUTH**” in downtown Kyiv this morning, I had two thoughts in quick succession:

First: interactive education should abandon kindergarten’s “everybody’s happy nowadays[1](#footnote-1)” approach and focus entirely on maps. Drop students into utterly alien environments—Chicago, Portland—armed only with digitized maps. Time how long it takes them to orient themselves in these baffling wayfinding systems, each more chaotic than the next. Then have them create accurate interactive experiences of these places and share them with the world.

Second: we should revive pulp fiction’s tradition of rescuing failing plots by adding labyrinths—except now they’re AI-generated mazes embedded in websites.

[Image]

Both thoughts dissolved when I scrolled to photographs of the not-very-portable cement bomb shelters being erected in the capital looking like brutalist sculptures on their way to an exhibition that no one wants to attend.

[Image]

I prefer the ones used in Odesa, which are more compact and aesthetically pleasing.

## Day 1325

*Date: 2025-10-10 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*Kyiv loses power*

Our *turning off the lights post* on [Day 1323](https://abdymok.substack.com/p/day-1323) was prophetic, because, er, Russia did just that early this morning at around 2 a.m..

A Russian drone smashed into the 17-storey residential building across from Zvyrinetska Metro in Pechersk district.

[Image]

The Left Bank looks dark.

The exact moment most of the city lost power was captured for posterity. I was outside witnessing the chaos. Thank god I took the stairs—elevators and blackouts mix about as well as my cooking and smoke detectors.

Back at the flat, our house guest, maybe a relative, a young woman whose fastidiousness could shame Marie Kondo, had taken refuge in the corridor—perfectly centered between two walls—deeply absorbed in whatever she was tapping on her phone. Even during a power outage, she’d apparently found the most structurally sound spot to swap notes about incoming missiles.

[Image]

---

Eyes glazed over approximately 20 seconds into this fucking *reportage*:

[Image]

Liberalism, last time I checked: a political tradition centered on individual rights, liberties, and pluralism, governed by the rule of law rather than personalized power, that makes space for diverse conceptions of the good life while respecting human dignity and agency. You know, the boring stuff.

Back in March, [I noted](https://abdymok.substack.com/p/day-1109) we were neck-deep in the mature phase of our era of negativity—The Golden Age of Ameri[ꓘ](https://discussions.apple.com/thread/8205179)a—that whole trajectory from Minsk (1997) to Moscow (2000) to Vova’s first Ukrainian adventure (2014). I wrote then that the current wave of illiberalism was destructive, not constructive, and that when coercion runs out of steam, the negative phase ends. Simple physics, really.

What I didn’t fully game out was how quickly the bulldozer would wake the dragons.

The administration risks rousing Popperian rabble-rousers: enemies of patrimonialism who led the charge after the so-called end of history, who’ve since retired or stayed abroad. Push them too far, and they may reunite like ex-revolutionaries in *One Battle After Another*, dusting off the destabilization playbooks that once rocked Belarus, Georgia, Ukraine, Yugoslavia, Romania, and beyond when the USSR imploded. They’ll all be dragons again—assuming they haven’t already dropped dead.

This changes things. I had figured twenty to forty years of negativity. But maybe there are “subjective factors” that could “flatten the wave before it crests.” These weathered practitioners of open society building *are* that breakwater: experienced, networked, deeply allergic to the resurrection of the old normal they spent their youth dismantling.

The question is whether their toolkit still works in an era vaccinated by social media algorithms against progress. The ‘90s revolutions succeeded partly because information asymmetries favored the insurgents. Today’s digital landscape—more surveilled, more manipulated, more fractured—is different. But these operators cut their teeth navigating the KGB, Securitate, and Stasi. Perhaps algorithms are just the next iteration of the surveillance state they’ve already beaten once.

Whether they succeed or fail, their re-emergence confirms something: this isn’t just another pendulum swing. It’s a genuine contest between incompatible visions, fought by people who remember when the stakes were existential and are old enough not to give a shit about consequences.

Which, if nothing else, should make for decent entertainment.

---

Have a great weekend!

## Day 1327

*Date: 2025-10-12 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*A building faces sunrise*

[Image]

This residential building in Kyiv’s Pechersk District was hit on its eastern facade - the side where residents watch the sunrise each morning. Directly across the street is a cafe that was once called Trump Cafe, but the owners renamed it Nolan because they felt Donald was wasting Ukraine’s time. And he is.

Thomas Peter took a nice photo from inside after the attack, looking down the hill at the Dnipro River. You can make out Mat’ Rodina in the distance. She is looking in the direction of Moscow. She is made of titanium and it would be a lot of work, maybe impossible, to reposition the head. They fixed her shield a couple of years ago, but it’s already rusting.

[Image]

I live right around the block.

[Image]

If this map is correct, Kyiv got hit on October 10 by Shaheds coming from both sides of the Dnipro—because why attack from one direction when you can be annoying from two? The drones do their signature move: loitering in lazy circles before committing to the nosedive, which makes pinpointing their launch sites a fun guessing game. The little blue lines streaking down from the north? Ballistic missiles, probably the North Korean party favors Russia’s been showing off lately.

Here’s Z mastering the art of complaining while insisting he’s *totally not complaining* about Kyiv’s spectacular failure to protect its central heating plants from getting wrecked, which plunged the city into hours-long blackouts.

“Take CHP-5, CHP-6. This isn’t just about air defense—we can’t waste Patriots on drones. What questions should we ask the mayor? Kyiv’s municipal enterprises? I could tell you what I think about all this. But I won’t, because we all have one war,” Z said Friday, radiating restrained exasperation.

He stressed that priority one is standing firm and defeating the enemy.

“We’ll do everything. Where local authorities can’t handle it, where the private sector has relevant stations—we still have to protect it all. One country, one people, and nobody cares whose station or whose property it is when the lights go out,” he added. *— Interfax-Ukraine, October 10, 2025*

Sigh. Same guy who spent six months in 2021 getting told—repeatedly, insistently, with charts and maps—that Russia would definitely invade, and kept telling us that there was nothing to worry about.

## Day 1328

*Date: 2025-10-13 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*Criminal Code Geopolitics*

[Image]

Yesterday the sun finally came out.

Sigh.

[Video: https://youtu.be/V\_483oIFQ5w]

Send the missiles.

[Video: https://youtu.be/-JUjjeUl7lo]

**Мир по понятиям, теперь с дронами** (The World According to “Understandings,” Now with Drones)

So here we have the Financial Times [breathlessly reporting](https://www.ft.com/content/f9f42c10-3a30-4ee1-aff7-3368dd831c8c) that Trump is helping Ukraine bomb Russian refineries, as if this represents some grand strategic pivot in the “rules-based international order” (правопорядок, который уже давно сдох/the rules-based order that’s been dead for ages).

Reading this through Pastukhov’s lens of “[бесправие силы и бессилие права](https://republic.ru/posts/116524?utm_source=republic.ru&utm_medium=rss&utm_campaign=all)” (force without law, law without force) is revealing. The article perfectly illustrates his thesis: we’ve officially entered the era where the law is completely бессильно (powerless), while сила (force) operates совершенно беззаконно (utterly lawlessly).

Notice the exquisite legal gymnastics: Washington “hasn’t acknowledged a direct role” in strikes on civilian energy infrastructure, but officials happily brief the FT about “route planning, altitude, timing and mission decisions.” It’s like watching someone rob a bank while insisting they’re just “sharing intelligence about vault vulnerabilities.” Классическое понятие (classic criminal understanding): I wasn’t there, but here’s how to crack the safe.

The Trump-Z phone call is particularly rich. Trump asks if Ukraine can strike Moscow, then says he’s “merely asking a question, not encouraging further killing” — after which, coincidentally, intelligence flows “with a new level of specificity.” Это же чистой воды базар (this is pure criminal negotiation): I’m not saying do it, but hypothetically speaking, if someone were to do it, here’s how...

Pastukhov would appreciate the supreme irony: the same America that built its entire post-1945 mythology on the Нюрнбергский трибунал (Nuremberg Tribunal) and “laws of war” is now openly coordinating strikes on civilian infrastructure (refineries, not military targets!), then clutching its pearls about “escalation” and “drawing us into conflict.”

The cognitive dissonance is staggering: We’re helping you attack their economy and “make them feel the pain,” but also we’re Very Concerned™ about escalation. Translation: право (law) has become pure декорация (decoration), while сила (force) does whatever it wants, wrapped in a thin membrane of plausible deniability.

And the closing act! Trump casually threatening Tomahawks like a gopnik (гопник/street thug) pulling out a knife: “Look, if this war doesn’t get settled, I’m sending Tomahawks.” Putin “does not need that.” As if international relations are now conducted via implicit угрозы (threats) in press scrums. Опять же — чистое понятие (again — pure criminal code): settle this “right” or I’ll send the boys.

Pastukhov’s thesis vindicated: we’re living in a world where сила абсолютно бесправна (force is completely lawless) — no Geneva Conventions, no sovereignty norms, just pure корпоративные разборки (corporate score-settling) between nuclear armed братки (criminal brothers). Meanwhile, право абсолютно бессильно (law is completely powerless) — existing only as propaganda for domestic consumption.

The beautiful irony? This article celebrates it! “Ukraine’s success!” Meanwhile, every norm that supposedly separated “us” from “them” lies smoldering in a Bashkortostan refinery fire.

Pastukhov writes about the death of the rules-based order. This article is basically the autopsy report, except nobody realizes the patient is dead. They’re still taking his temperature while he decomposes.

**Понятийная геополитика** (Criminal Code Geopolitics): Now available with precision-guided munitions! 🚀

The perfect [sonic metaphor](https://www.google.com/search?q=aklou+ict+lyrics&oq=aklou+ict+lyrics&gs_lcrp=EgZjaHJvbWUyBggAEEUYOTIJCAEQABgNGIAEMggIAhAAGBYYHjIHCAMQABjvBdIBCjE4NjE0ajBqMTWoAgCwAgA&sourceid=chrome&ie=UTF-8) for this geopolitical moment.

[Embedded content: w.soundcloud.com]

Pumping sweet tunes (democracy! rules-based order! international law!) while accelerating toward... what exactly? “Tomorrow’s never/So I go faster”—that’s the logic of escalation without endgame.

Everyone’s chasing the ice cream truck:

- America: *“I need the answer/It’s in the flavours”*—searching for the magic combination of weapons/sanctions/intelligence that “solves” Ukraine
- [China](http://washingtonpost.com/world/2025/10/13/china-russia-drone-parts-ukraine/?itid=sr_1_74a84a09-dc29-4806-a6bf-13264008eaf7): Pumping components to Russia, vanilla-flavored neutrality masking strawberry-red support
- [Poland](https://www.wsj.com/world/europe/as-russian-aggression-turns-west-poland-says-its-ready-328a0a13): *“Faster than I have ever gone”*—210,000 troops, can’t stop now
- Russia: Driver who won’t pull over because tomorrow’s never

The exhaustion is palpable: *“I beg you to stop soon/Cause I cannot run anymore”*. Ukraine’s scrambling to keep up with Russian fiber-optic drones. Europe’s fiscal limits meeting Polish military ambitions. The entire sanctions regime chasing Chinese supply chains that just... keep... going.

And that haunting line: *“And you’re the keeper of it all”*—who’s driving this thing? Trump with his Tomahawk threats? Xi with his component exports? Putin with his territorial ambitions? Or is the truck driverless, just momentum and inertia pumping sweet tunes while everyone runs behind?

Bright packaging (strawberry! vanilla! democracy! values!) moving faster than anyone can sustain, and nobody knows how to stop it or even if there’s a driver anymore.

*“Tomorrow’s never”*—so we just go faster. That’s the autopsy in four words.

Which brings me to the credibility *snapback* theatre Samuel and Jeremy presented last week, apropos “credible” Ukraine security guarantees: automatic sanctions, weapons surges, financial stabilization funds—all triggered within 48 hours if Russia violates a cease-fire**[1](#footnote-1).**

One problem: legal mechanisms in a lawless world.

**The sanctions fantasy:** China just sold Russia 328,000 miles of fiber-optic cable in a month. Beijing ignores every sanction while NATO writes concerned statements. Charap wants to make this *automatic*? Russia’s already under comprehensive sanctions—they forced Moscow deeper into Chinese/Iranian partnerships that operate entirely outside Western frameworks. You can’t “automatically” reimpose something that never worked.

**The weapons mirage:** The FT [showed](https://www.ft.com/content/f9f42c10-3a30-4ee1-aff7-3368dd831c8c) America *already* providing “route planning, altitude, timing” for Ukrainian strikes through backchannels. Trump *already* threatens Tomahawks. Charap wants to codify what exists precisely *because* formal commitments are impossible. You can’t institutionalize force without law—you just get more lawless force with paperwork.

**The trigger trap:** Russia fires a drone over the border. Ukraine claims violation. European foreign ministers have 48 hours to decide, or sanctions auto-trigger? This isn’t deterrence—it’s a hair-trigger for re-escalation that Russia will never accept and would test constantly if it did.

**The core delusion:** Samuel and Jeremy admit NATO won’t fight Russia, then propose... *different promises*. As if credibility comes from institutional design rather than political will. Poland hits fiscal limits at 4.7% GDP. Europe’s economy stagnates. American support swings with elections. No “standing fund” changes this.

The patient is dead. Stop designing better thermometers.

A lot of europium, by the way, comes from the [Bayan Obo mine](https://earth.google.com/web/search/mine+near+Bayan+Obo,+Baotou,+Inner+Mongolia,+China/@41.83076918,109.97534639,1633.75278426a,75254.68207142d,35y,360h,0t,0r/data=CqEBGnMSbQolMHgzNjA0OTViZmUyMzQ1OTU3OjB4ZGZiZjQ4NDAzMTk3OWU3ZRlDiNCtfOJEQCGD3bBtUX5bQCoybWluZSBuZWFyIEJheWFuIE9ibywgQmFvdG91LCBJbm5lciBNb25nb2xpYSwgQ2hpbmEYASABIiYKJAmCi_S4t1tEQBFhhbD0i1tEQBk6ko8_YXpSwCFs5avMlXpSwEICCAE6AwoBMEICCABKDQj___________8BEAA) in inner Mongolia. Historically, the U.S. had **small europium stocks** — for example, from the Mountain Pass operation before the 1990s, when it was still under [Molycorp](https://www.molycorp.com/) and produced some europium oxide for red phosphors in color TVs. But that production ended decades ago.

[Traxys](https://www.traxys.com/) was a financial/inventory partner and early investor in the reboot of [Molycorp](https://www.molycorp.com/) (Mountain Pass). They helped buy the Mountain Pass assets in 2008, provided inventory-financing and offtake arrangements, took an equity stake, helped sponsor the 2010 IPO — and then largely exited/sold their interests by about 2013.

Considar Inc. was one of the legacy firms that merged into and formed the core of the Traxys trading platform in 2003. Joe (Joseph) Filner founded and operated Considar — it was one of several metals-trading companies he ran (alongside Newmet Corp. / Noblemet and Project Development Inc.). He retired in the 1990s and died in 2000. We all remember his son [Bob](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bob_Filner), who in 2013 *self-destructed* as a misogynist US Congressman from San Diego. He died in April.

I met him a couple of times at the [Algonquin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Algonquin_Hotel) before the bar was *revitalized*. That’s where I learned all about ball bearings, which are made from high-carbon chromium steels, and magnet powders.

## Day 1331 

*Date: 2025-10-16 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*Tragedy as timeline *

[Video: https://youtu.be/tV1\_0UB24Sw]

General Keane explains why Team USA should fork over ground-launched Tomahawk missiles, yesterday.

But Trump is worried about Putin’s image, as if the goal of the exercise is to burnish his democratic credentials.

> “[The war] doesn’t make him [Putin] look good.
>
> And all we want from President Putin is stop this. Stop killing Ukrainians and stop killing Russians, because he’s killing a lot of Russians. Again, it doesn’t make him look good. It’s a war that he should have won in one week, and he’s now going into its fourth year.” — [Trump](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=f1M57bKXlKU) (White House, October 15, 2025)

The war is *not* going into its fourth year. We are *in* the middle of the twelfth year.

[Image]

**The Formula That Doesn’t Scale**

Military aid collapsed 43% by summer 2025, right after NATO invented PURL. Eight NATO countries scraped together €1.9 billion for “ready-to-use” weapons. Europe cut its own support by 57%.

Yet… forty percent of Russia’s refining capacity down. Queues stretching kilometers. Crimea rationing fuel at 30 liters per person. By August, 16 of Russia’s 38 refineries struck. Swarms of 300 drones on single targets. Over a million barrels per day offline.

The Americans? “Not acknowledging a direct role.” Just sharing intelligence on targets, altitudes, timing. After Trump’s July call with Z—”could you hit Moscow?”—US intelligence flowed with “new specificity.” Route planning. Air defense mapping. Washington’s “instrument” for strangling the war machine. It works: Russia now imports fuel from Belarus and China.

We’ll be talking about the war with him [Z] and we’ll be talking about -- I mean, they [Ukraine] want to go offensive. **I’ll make a determination on that.** But they would like to go offensive, you know that, and we’ll have to make a determination. - [Trump](https://rollcall.com/factbase/trump/transcript/donald-trump-press-conference-fbi-octoberr-15-2025/) (October 15, 2025)

Now Ukraine shops in Washington. ATACMS. HIMARS. Drone technology sharing. Energy resilience. Weapons procurement dressed as negotiation prep. This is the actual agenda.

Trump, fresh off Gaza, is “optimistic” he can solve Ukraine too. The formula worked—pressure weakened Netanyahu, Hamas folded, ceasefire announced. Surely it scales to Russia?

No. Putin isn’t Netanyahu. He’s not democratically accountable. He’s not desperate for a lifeline.

The White House says Russia “should be motivated” by economic pain. Shambles economy. Virtually no territorial gains. But pain and capitulation aren’t the same thing. Russia is bleeding and still fighting because Putin cannot justify a million soldiers dead or dismembered for nothing. Victory is the only acceptable outcome. Anything less is catastrophic political failure.

Trump doesn’t understand this. He sees weakness and expects compromise. But Putin sees existential stakes.

The tragedy is simpler than anyone admits: Ukraine identified the solution in 2022. Close the 20-fold gap in strike capability or attrition wins. It took until 2025 to act. Now they’re calling it diplomacy. What it actually is: three years late, and framed as leverage for negotiations that were probably never coming anyway.

## Day 1332 

*Date: 2025-10-17 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*Slip and dip *

[Image]

On the day the world ends

Women walk through the fields under their umbrellas,

A drunkard grows sleepy at the edge of a lawn,

Vegetable peddlers shout in the street

And a yellow-sailed boat comes nearer the island,

The voice of a violin lasts in the air

And leads into a starry night.

And those who expected lightning and thunder

Are disappointed.

And those who expected signs and archangels’ trumps

Do not believe it is happening now.

As long as the sun and the moon are above,

As long as the bumblebee visits a rose,

As long as rosy infants are born

No one believes it is happening now[1](#footnote-1).

That’s part of of a lovely poem by Czeslaw Milosz, whose experiences in Vilnius and Poland during World War II and under Commust rule didn’t keep him from having a great time in California and becoming an American citizen. A national treasure.

It’s been a poetry and music week. Which brings me to [Frank O’Hara](https://x.com/frankohara_) and [Memoir of Sergei O](https://www.cityartsmagazine.com/poetry-public/), in [Lunch Poems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunch_Poems), his first book.

Anyway, there is a line…

[Image]

You are ruining your own country and me

I [listened](https://www.fdd.org/team/radm-ret-mark-montgomery/) to Mark’s sitrep this morning, about new missile systems—while playing rapid chess (lost two close games in quick succession).

[Video: https://youtu.be/t3xzXb0ftjI]

**We’re in the 12th year of the war**. It started out as Putin against western civilization but has morphed into Europe vs. Russia, with Trump now trying to play arms supplier/umpire. **Not the right approach.** Because China, North Korea, Iran, Venezuela, Cuba, Hungary, Belarus, Slovakia—all the illiberal wackos who are helping Russia to kill us—support Putin.

Always worth repeating:

- **Phase 1** ended on March 25, 2022 Day 30, after Putin failed to decapitate Kyiv[2](https://abdymok.substack.com/p/phase-2-ends?utm_source=publication-search#footnote-2-53858406).
- **Phase 2** ended on May 8, 2022, Day 74, after Putin failed to gobble up Donbas[3](https://abdymok.substack.com/p/phase-2-ends?utm_source=publication-search#footnote-3-53858406).
- **Phase 3** will drag on until Ukraine is de-occupied.
- **Phase 4** will never end.

I think it was John Maynard Keynes who said, “In the long run, we are all dead.[2](#footnote-2)”
