>
> I can’t shake the feeling that world leaders are, how shall I put it, not entirely honest with Volodymyr Z. I reject the idea that he’s simply naïve and invents unattainable plans out of thin air. I think he’s being offered advances - political IOUs, promissory gestures he clings to like straws. And those straws keep breaking, one after another. Yet he never gives up and keeps reaching for the next one.
>
> To be fair, that’s exactly how he’s managed to stay afloat - from straw to straw, for three years now. By any expert’s calculation, he should have drowned long ago. And yet he’s still treading water. The trouble is, you can’t rise on that - you can only keep floundering, waiting for the sun to evaporate the water so you can finally push off the bottom. Unless, of course, something knocks from below again.
>
> The Tomahawk story is just the latest straw. The core of the problem is simple: from the very beginning, it was made clear to Z that Europe (and, back then, America as well) was ready to help him fight - but not to fight for him, and certainly not with him.
>
> That formula needs to stay in mind whenever we discuss the realism or wishfulness of any new promise. The West - and by that, I mean both sides of the Atlantic - will do absolutely nothing that could even remotely risk being drawn into this war alongside or instead of Ukraine.
>
> And they won’t do it for one plain reason: not a single Western leader, not even the most hawkish Baltics or Poles, has a public mandate for war. What they have, at best, is a mandate to support Ukraine. And even that is increasingly questioned - both from the right and the left.
>
> What can actually be expected are two things.
>
> First, dancing on the edge - cautiously probing where exactly the line lies beyond which Moscow would strike not at Ukraine, but elsewhere. (A dangerous but exhilarating game, great for adrenaline junkies.)
>
> Second, attempts to provoke some third party into doing what you don’t want to do yourself. Europe still dreams this could become “Trump’s war.” Trump, on the other hand, wishes the Europeans all possible success in making it theirs.
>
> The Tomahawk case is even simpler.
>
> Russia already has weapons roughly in the same class - Kalibr, for example, and the U.S. already has plenty of enemies besides Moscow. So the obvious counterstep would be to deliver “symmetrical” weapons to some freedom-loving seafarers in Yemen or defenders of democracy in Venezuela. Biden has, in fact, already been warned about this scenario.
>
> But that’s just an illustration. Moscow still has plenty of ways to make a mess - that’s an objective reality, palpable and undeniable, yet one the West is still unwilling to internalise. Unfortunately, that doesn’t make it any less real. Which is why the outcome of Z’s Washington visit was entirely predictable: there will be no Tomahawks. There will be nothing that poses a real threat to Putin’s regime, because if that regime ever faces the prospect of losing power, it’s capable of anything. And every Western leader, including Trump, knows this, or at least pretends to.
>
> The bottom line:
>
> Ukraine will not be allowed a swift end to this war - no knockout, not even a knockdown. She’s being steered toward a long match, where the best possible outcome is a win on points, if she lasts until the final round.
>
> The more I watch, the more convinced I am: Trump will not fight Putin. He’ll either twist Z’s arm or simply wash his hands of him. — [Telegram](https://t.me/pastukhov_eng/14) (October 19, 2025)

## Day 1338

*Date: 2025-10-23 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*Psycopathic strategic ambivalence*

[Image]

A friend from Dnipro gave me a gigantic piece of cake. A small piece is on the plate. Almonds on top, some carrots and dried fruits. A little heavier than I expected, but 9 on a cake scale of 1 to 10.

Other than a brief stretch in Hrodna and a few months in the center of Warsaw (we didn’t quite get along), I lived in Minsk for almost four years. I liked it there.

[Image]

Late one night about 30 years ago I was coming home in Minsk on the bridge over the Svislach River. I was feeling, oh, *moderately* misanthropic, and eager to get home to bed. After swinging around into the park from the long curve by Victory Square, traffic suddenly came to a dead halt. I was boxed in on four sides by police with truncheons and shields. After several minutes of bewilderment, I saw that cars were held up not because of something terrible like an accident, or merely inconvenient like an opposition march: it was a bunch of teenagers, who had emptied bags of glass marbles at the top of the hill.

And thus began my long and progressively more hostile relationship with law-enforcers in general.

# An Annotated Manifesto Against Tidying Up Joy

Marie, or KonMarie as she insists we call her (because one name wasn’t quite guru enough), has graced Google again this year with wisdom about finding joy at work. Her previous appearance concerned the radical notion of cleaning up after yourself—riveting stuff for a company worth billions.

In a TIME interview, we recall, she revealed keeping a musical tuner at home. When it rings, she claims, it purifies the air and calms her mind. One might as well keep a toaster. It makes toast. Same principle.

Here’s what she won’t tell you about that tuner: Musical scales themselves expose the fraud of perfect order. Between any two notes lies infinite possibility, yet humans choose discrete pitches—the five-note pentatonic, the seven-note diatonic, the twelve-tone chromatic. These emerge not from tidiness but from physical resonance: strings vibrating at 3/2 ratios, creating the pleasure we call consonance.

Our elaborate system was cultural choice, not cosmic law. We learned that in Solfeggio.

The tuner rings at 440 Hz—A above middle C—but only because we agreed in 1939. It’s arbitrary. Organized. Tidy.

Real music—real joy—lives in embodied knowledge, not imposed order. You can’t spark-joy your way to Oklou. On the occasion of yet another genuflection before the tidy person (that sound you hear is techno-fascist knees hitting floor), remember: the tuner only knows one note. Music requires all of them, messy and glorious and true.

This is something to think about when you are walking up eight flights of stairs.

## Day 1342

*Date: 2025-10-27 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*The absurdist war economy*

Don’t take the elevator.

[Image]

# The Absurdist War Economy

So a casting agency pivots to drones and suddenly it’s got $1 billion in contracts. Fire Point went from scouting film locations for Z’s rom-coms to churning out exploding Styrofoam birds at thirty secret factories. The owner still runs a location scouting business. The CTO used to make concrete patio furniture. Now they’re building the Flamingo—a jet-powered drone with a one-ton warhead that got its name because they ran out of red paint. Z promises 3,000 of them this year. You cannot make this up[1](#footnote-1).

Meanwhile, Putin’s playing the long game of making Ukraine uninhabitable. His new Shahed UAV’s—now three times faster, AI-guided, dive-bombing from above—come in waves of 600 or 700 a night. They’re systematically dismantling the power grid, substation by substation, trying to cut the industrial east off from energy production in the west. The goal isn’t conquest anymore. It’s depopulation. Make it so cold and dark that millions simply leave.

Ukraine’s counter-strategy is the mirror image: blow up Russia’s refineries until the economy collapses first. Fire Point claims its drones now conduct 60% of Ukraine’s strikes inside Russia. Fuel shortages are spreading. But there’s a catch—most drones get intercepted, so you need volume, which means keeping them cheap, which apparently means tolerating quality issues and skipping price negotiations with companies run by people who made garden benches last year.

Under the diplomatic carpet, the bulldogs gnaw. Putin wants Z gone—not the man, the symbol. Trump doesn’t want to be Putin’s errand boy but also doesn’t want to fund this forever. So they circle the airfield, unable to land, while Fire Point paints another batch of Flamingos and Kyiv’s metro goes dark for the first time ever.

By January, this war will have lasted longer than the Soviet Union fought Nazi Germany. Russia’s conquered an additional 1% of Ukraine at the cost of a million casualties. The drone revolution means neither side can make major gains. So they’ll just keep bombing each other’s infrastructure until somebody’s economy collapses first.

This is Kyiv’s Botanical Gardens early Saturday morning. The park sits above Vydubychi Monastery, which dates to the 11th century when Vsevolod I was in charge. This was during the height of Kievan Rus’ power, though the state was beginning to experience the internal conflicts and succession disputes that would eventually lead to its fragmentation

The political structure was complex, with various princes controlling different cities and territories, but Kyiv remained the most prestigious seat and its ruler held the title of Grand Prince. The peak of Kyivan Rus empire started crumbling in the middle of the 12th century.

> **The War of Exhaustion**
>
> Gone are the dreams of a victory parade on Khreshchatyk or a cup of coffee on Yalta’s embankment. The war is now about something else entirely – about who will collapse first from sheer exhaustion.
>
> [@ZUa](https://x.com/ZUa) believes in long-range missiles; [#Putin](https://x.com/hashtag/Putin?src=hashtag_click) still believes in Gerasimov and in the submission of the Russian people, ready to die without complaint in endless “meat assaults.” Europe believes in money, and that it will have enough to grind down, with Ukrainian hands, as many Russians as Putin can herd to the front. The real resource war is only beginning now. Welcome to its new phase – one run not by parade-ground generals, but by rear-echelon draft officers (in [#Ukraine](https://x.com/hashtag/Ukraine?src=hashtag_click), the TCC chiefs), a world of blackouts and petrol queues, drug shortages, and endless nighttime sirens on both sides of the front line. In fact, this is now the central front line of the war. We don’t know precisely what Z and the Europeans told Trump, but it seems they convinced him that this “second front” could be broken through. I can find no other explanation for Washington’s return to the concept of “peace after a ceasefire.”
>
> Let’s set aside, for now, the question of what exactly triggered [@realDonaldTrump](https://x.com/realDonaldTrump), and look instead at what underlies the confidence of Z and the Europeans that they can win this war:
>
> 1. The assumption that Europe can afford, economically and politically, to finance the war in Ukraine for several more years.
>
> 2. The assumption that Z will be able, over those same years, to keep replenishing the Armed Forces with manpower through mobilisation.
>
> 3. The assumption that Trump, at Europe’s request, will be able to sustain sanctions long enough to keep constraining Russia’s oil and gas revenues, leading to a slow but inevitable degradation of the Russian economy.
>
> 4. Europe has entirely reoriented itself toward a long-term resource war – and feels comfortable enough on its fronts to have shaken Trump’s conviction that Russia cannot be defeated.
>
> And what about [#Russia](https://x.com/hashtag/Russia?src=hashtag_click)? The [#Kremlin](https://x.com/hashtag/Kremlin?src=hashtag_click), it seems, has also adapted. It clearly understands the seriousness of the threat and, having revised its earlier optimistic assumption on the fly, is coming up with a new one – the only thing better than an ongoing war is the next war. The one not yet fought. Either way, a prolonged resource war with the collective West is not the kind of war the Kremlin dreamed of. As was [@MedvedevRussiaE](https://x.com/MedvedevRussiaE) prophetic words: “They’ll fuss for a while and then get used to it – everything will go back to normal.” Reaching the point of no return in relations with the West was never part of the plan. So, when asked whether Putin is ready to fight forever, I can now answer with some certainty: no.
>
> Right now, Putin genuinely wants to wind down the war in Ukraine as soon as possible – to wriggle out of an open-ended resource war with the West. But how? Two paths are obvious:
>
> 1. To win the resource war with Ukraine before he begins to lose the resource war with Europe. This means that military pressure on Ukraine will soon triple, regardless of human, financial, or reputational losses. In the coming months, the fighting will reach unprecedented brutality – an actual Doomsday war. But if Ukraine holds out under this onslaught (through spring 2026), I won’t venture to predict what happens next.
>
> 2. Moscow will focus its maximum effort on making Europe feel the weight of war and rethink its approach to funding Ukraine. There will be no invasion – simply because, with the army bogged down in Ukraine, there’s no one left to invade with – but every known form of hybrid warfare will be unleashed in the coming months.
>
> So I wouldn’t dismiss Putin’s promise of an “earth-shaking response.” The Kremlin knows full well that the war has reached a decisive moment – and it has no desire to let it drag on in a sluggish, low-intensity form. Ukrainian forces rotate units, build command posts for instant evacuation, and destroy buildings not to *win*, but to kill as many Russians as possible. The 400 km² Pokrovsk–Myrnohrad agglomeration has been diced into sectors on Palantir maps while start-ups test new warfighting tech. Western flaks say the battle for Pokrovsk could be a milestone. Reality calls it more ruin made manifest, a theater of perfidy, maps, drones and rotting corpses. One big fat grey kill zone.

## Day 1354

*Date: 2025-11-08 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*Predictive text*

[Image]

It is Saturday and DTEK was supposed to have turned ***on*** the electricity one hour ago. I’m using my phone as a hotspot and want a cup of coffee, maybe two, but the thought of walking down eight flights of stairs and visiting Space Coffee a couple of blocks away makes me feel ill. There is another alternative: the new 1950 bystro (that’s its name — ”1950”), but I’m not sure they have a generator.

I stick my head out the window to look at the neighborhood. No bright lights anywhere. Lots of grey.

For some reason, Substack this morning wants to end my sentences for me. The UI is serving up various options automatically. The predictive text pop ups are annoying.

Let’s see if I can figure how to turn this *tool*… off.

Turns out the the feature migrated to the computer because I’m connected via phone. I killed it, permanently.

Where was I? Ah, blackout.

The DTEK website just [told](https://www.dtek-kem.com.ua/ua/shutdowns) me emergency *stabilization* outages in Kyiv will persist, er, until 1430, maybe.

[Image]

What should I do?

- It’s perfect running weather — 9C
- Continue tweaking code for [abdymok.tumblr.com](https://abdymok.tumblr.com/)
- Sort out my clothes
- Read a book

Maybe all of the above.

Turns out Russia killed a bunch of people in Dnipro during the overnight air attack.

A Shahed UAV smashed into a residential building, killing three, including two kids.

## Day 1356

*Date: 2025-11-10 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*Blackouts are not normal*

[Lights flicker in Kyiv.](https://www.theguardian.com/world/2025/nov/09/volodymyr-Z-why-should-i-be-afraid-donald-trump) Z grins. “It’s normal,” he says, framing blackouts as quaint quirks. Every word polished, every gesture rehearsed. He denies tension with Trump, praises King Charles, radiates calm moral authority. *“So why should we be afraid?”*

[Image]

It’s “normal” because the president and his top managers failed miserably to prepare. Same with fall-back lines of defense, fortifications, mandatory military conscription, selective justice (persecution of political rivals), etc., ad nauseum. This might explain why anti-corruption agencies this morning [are searching](https://nv.ua/ukr/ukraine/politics/nabu-priyshlo-z-obshukami-do-germana-galushchenka-ta-v-energoatom-novini-ukrajini-50559334.html) the residence of Ukraine’s Justice Minister Herman Halushchenko.

[The Guardian](https://www.theguardian.com/world/ukraine) isn’t writing about that. It’s all hush hush.

Meanwhile, pockets collapse in the Pokrovsk-Myrnohrad agglomeration. Russian and Ukrainian forces grind through access roads, probing and interdicting each other’s GLOCs. Drone swarms hum, mortars boom, positions shift by meters, weather frustrates operations. Russian advances have slowed; reinforcements trickle in. Uncertainty dominates, indifferent to heroics or cameras.

[Image]

Z’s show‑business polish conceals logistics, attrition, and the slow grind of reality. He has finally recognized this is a war of stalemate, not attrition, something we pointed out laboriously on Day 1323[1](#footnote-1).

The president is a comedian of slapstick idiocy: timing, gestures, moral punchlines, all carefully rehearsed. Standup itself, much like his set, pleads: *please like me, please like me.* Polished, heroic, consumable.

Maybe replace him with Eddie Izzard, who didn’t mask absurdity but exposed it. We don’t want to conceal the grind. It’s better to illuminate it without the theatrics.

[Image]

I direct your attention to the pledge page at **ukraine@war**. The backoffice is now run by someone representing herself as the the terribly sane Japanese organizer Marie Kondo, aka [KonMarie](https://konmari.com/?srsltid=AfmBOopueSUIqjASRZS3HDYhEaJc_znxxu8OeBXT4kMpCr9Dz2QzyUSB), who has hooked it up to [Stripe](http://stripe.com) in order to pay for portable charging devices like the one on the table below[2](#footnote-2). She will also use pledges to pay off debts from her MBA vacation in the United States.

[Image]

I have great hopes for the site becoming a reliable source of comment and information freed of the sentimental histrionics and pre‑emptive ridicule of any opposing view germane to warblogs, most of which have gone from licking each other’s wounds following the start of hostilities, to taking grim pleasure cataloguing every atrocity carried out in the name of preserving empire, to forming—with a sort of shuddering inevitability—into a lockstep pro Z mosh pit in which the most cynical predictions of last year—about those who would dare to advance a political agenda on the back of inconceivable human tragedy—have become not only true, but somehow above criticism.

Conversely, I expect [ukraine@war](https://abdymok.substack.com/) to be free of the handwringing that characterizes some Substack authors, among whom, now and again, a flurry of concern arises over What Must Be Done about whatever bile currently poisons the postings and their comment sections. It shouldn’t be in the least surprising that idiots driven by bloodlust and malformed patriotism are sharing their opinions on X every chance they get. Yes, there is unchecked bootlicking, great crimson swathes of it. Same as it ever was. Let them rant and screed and ‘fisk’ and bleat. As with any echo chamber—[Iuliia Mendel’s Weekly Updates](https://substack.com/@iuliiamendel?utm_source=global-search) included—no one outside it is listening.

What will be made evident, I hope, is that criticism of Ukraine’s leadership failures is not a reflexive ideological matter, born out of political allegiances, pro-Russian sympathies, or what colour underwear one prefers. It is rather awareness that we can condemn both: Putin’s criminal invasion—a genocidal war of aggression driven by his maniacal need to control resources and power, a bloodthirsty calculus in which Ukrainian lives are merely variables in his delusions of empire—and simultaneously Z’s catastrophic failure to prepare adequate defenses, protect critical infrastructure, or manage this war with anything approaching competence.

As opposed, let’s say, to the prevailing narrative that demands we choose between uncritical worship of Z’s wartime leadership or tacit support for Russian aggression. Both can be true. Both must be said. One does not cancel the other, no matter how much the cheerleaders and the defeatists wish it did.

## Day 1357

*Date: 2025-11-11 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*The impersonator*

Russia has discovered a cost-effective recruitment strategy: empty the prisons, hand the inmates some two-wheeled transport, and aim them at Ukrainian cities. The result is [Pokrovsk](https://deepstatemap.live/#13/48.2823453/37.1719925) under siege by what looks like a post-apocalyptic biker gang, except less organized and more genocidal. Call it a frontier epic for the brain-poisoned: society’s castoffs find meaning in life by crossing borders on scooters to eliminate strangers. The Kremlin calls it patriotism. Everyone else calls it a war crime on wheels. (Video c/o [bmpd](https://t.me/bmpd_cast/24280))

Dobropillia, Kostiantynivka, Druzhivka are next, followed by Kramatorsk and Slovyansk.

[Image]

---

So that’s the external threat: genocidal bikers on a border-crossing field trip. Now for the internal one, which is less Mad Max and more clownish, but just as corrosive[1](#footnote-1).

The president’s former business partner and birthday buddy, Tymur Mindich, fled the country yesterday hours before anti-corruption agents raided dozens of offices and residences as part of a 15-month long investigation into an elaborate kickback scheme run by an organized criminal group. Former Energy Minister, Herman Halushchenko, currently Ukraine’s Justice Minister, is a suspect. Har, har[2](#footnote-2).

There are more than **a thousand hours** of recorded conversations backing up the charges, investigators [said](https://t.me/nab_ukraine/3504)[3](#footnote-3).

That’s not a data point. It’s a mountain range.

**One thousand hours** equals **sixty thousand minutes**. Conversational Russian—the language that they speak—runs around 180 words per minute, but only about seventy percent of that time is active speech—people pause, interrupt, repeat, mumble, smoke. Do the math and you get roughly 42,000 minutes of actual talking, or about **7.5 million words**.

That’s thirteen *War and Peaces*, or eighty-four average novels—all dictated by men who assumed no one was listening.

And that’s *before* transcription. Even with software and 4× playback, it would take months to process. Listening is one thing; understanding is another. The work isn’t to *hear* the words—it’s to hear what they mean, in context, through the evasions and half-sentences.

Anyone who has ever tried to transcribe a real conversation knows that speech is mud. Overlaps, fillers, grunts, the sound of a chair scraping—everything matters. Meaning hides in rhythm and omission. A “hmm” can signal agreement, contempt, or the start of an heart attack.

Now imagine **seven and a half million** of those words, traded between men who know when not to say the *thing* itself. What sounds like boredom may be fear; what sounds like a joke may be an order.

Western analysts during the early 2000s failed miserably to keep pace with Kuchma’s conversational jazz. Two decades later, the problem isn’t comprehension—it’s scale. The signal hasn’t vanished; it’s drowned in abundance.

Until those thousand hours are released, we can only picture the investigators listening again and again, headphones on, mapping the contours of corruption—one strategic state enterprise at a time.

## Day 1358 

*Date: 2025-11-12 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*Che *

[Image]

Ukrainians can’t afford Z — because he and his friends have already looted the place. Not figuratively. Literally.

Does anyone remember Andriy Naumov? I don’t know if we’ll bother with Tymur Mindich and Operation Midas. We already know how this ends: Z and his top managers will be gone when the war’s over — just not to bombed out cities, and not to prison.

It’s remarkable what people outside Ukraine are *not* allowed to see and understand. Television news producers and newspaper editors have quietly decided that the total obliteration of cities in Donbas is bad for ratings—or maybe just bad for the nerves. So the footage stays politely tucked away. This fifteen-second clip, though, doesn’t care about decorum: a FAB-1500 and its bigger sibling, the FAB-3000, erase an entire high-rise in Myrnohrad, Donetsk region. The Su-34s that drop them cruise at 33,000 feet, forty kilometers back from the front, safe enough to bomb civilians with bureaucratic detachment. Each impact is an editorial decision someone chose not to show you.

It’s the same with Operation Midas.

[Image]

Ukrainians have known for years that foreign linguistic experts pick up on much less in conversation than native speakers. However, few expected that the time would come when the standard of competency turns out to be the ability to construe accurately what have been officially classified as state secrets.

While the participants in conversations allegedly recorded by the National Anti-corruption Bureau of Ukraine (NABU) seem to understand what they are saying, foreign specialists—including reporters of foreign-based media based in Kyiv—are still struggling to keep up, at least professionally.

Much is taken for granted between the speakers recorded, whoever they really are, and important communications take place without explicit linguistic reference. to the uninformed and tin-eared, the flow of words seems continuous, despite clear markers of the boundaries of meaningful information.

Professional transcribers know, of course, that listening comprehension is a classic *learn-by-doing* task, in which it is essential to adopt the strategy of listening for key elements around which to construct meaning, all the while moving along with the flow of discourse.

In reading mode, difficulties can readily be solved by rescanning the text, since the reader controls how information reaches the eyes. but listening is quite different.

Those familiar with the recordings cannot effectively ask decision-makers in the West to *listen faster*, nor will study of non-normative speech per se produce direct improvement in their ability to hear at the rate suspects in the Midas Operation speak.

Western analysts who have listened to the conversations are either finding it enormously difficult to understand what is actually being said or are playing dumb while busily compartmentalizing the intelligence for future use.

In any case, forensic linguists employed by [NABU](https://nabu.gov.ua/en/) this time are helping locals comprehend what was allegedly said, by whom and when.

In ukraine just like anywhere else, people interrupt each other and talk at the same time, ambient noise masks speech sounds, and politicians do not pronounce words as consistently as the written language would lead us to believe. but missing (and superfluous) acoustic signals are more than compensated for by situational and contextual elements in the conversations.

That some *hear* what may be obscured or totally lacking in the recordings is principally because a significant portion of what can be understood is supplied by the context of the exchanges. Native speakers draw on vast amounts of shared knowledge to construct meaning when they are listening. It is not so much that the speech sounds *contain* meaning; it is rather that they *trigger* meaning.

As an example, consider this dialogue, dated February 2, 2025, in which [now former] Deputy Prime Minister of Ukraine and Minister Deputy Prime Minister of Ukraine — [Minister of National Unity of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_National_Unity_of_Ukraine) Oleksiy Chernyshov shows up a a medical clinic to collect a bag full of cash.

Here is a partially translated transcript of the actual exchange, which evidently took place on February 2, 2025.

[Image]

Several important details are missing and there are some transcription mistakes, but maybe we’ll fill them in/fix them later.

[Image]

The informed listener can easily fill in the \*\*\*\* , as well as other missing elements in the recordings, by assuming the speakers know what they are talking about, even though they do not elaborate that knowledge in depth, e.g. *хачапурі*.

The ability to fill in missing information, a commonplace in human behavior, has been termed *analysis by synthesis* by psychologists. it refers to the strategy of internally generating or *shadowing* what people say (or do) so that we may more easily reduce the number of possibilities to consider.

Policy makers in Europe and the United States should take the time and make full use of their processing capacity to thoroughly examine the contents of available recordings made at Hrushevskoho 9A and other locations in central Kyiv. As long as their ignorance is bliss, our future will remain static.

## Day 1359 

*Date: 2025-11-13 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*ხაჭაპური*

[Image]

# Thug Life

**November 2021**: Journalist Yuriy Butusov after Z’s five-hour presser: “I should have stood up and punched him in the face.” Only respect for the office held him back. We wrote then that Z had driven Ukraine into a double-tracked curve where the rear wheel bogey jumped the rails—flattening the economy while failing to prevent civilian deaths. Wagnergate, helicopter taxi services via the State Emergency Service, constant allegations of impropriety. “We must pull the lever,” Z said. But he was the trolley problem[1](#footnote-1).

[Image]

.**November 2025**: Z briefs US senators—Graham, Shaheen, Blumenthal, Klobuchar—on Pokrovsk, air defense, sanctions, abducted children. Credibility required.

Also Wednesday: His Kvartal 95 co-founder Tymur Mindich flees hours before investigators arrive with wiretaps documenting a $100 million nuclear kickback scheme. Thursday brings more exits: energy regulator Serhiy Pushkar slips out Tuesday night. Businessman Alexander Zuckerman vanishes.

They called it “slagbaum”—Dutch for “barrier.” Perfect: borrow a Low Countries word for your shakedown while begging Brussels for €140 billion in frozen Russian assets. Force Energoatom contractors to pay 10-15% or face blocked payments. Missiles destroyed the grid; insiders monetized the rubble.

Ukraine’s worst winter since tanks rolled toward Kyiv: February insolvency, Pokrovsk falling, 60% of gas capacity destroyed, plus “slagbaum.”

Investigators drop 1,000 hours of wiretaps as Brussels debates that reparations loan—blocked by Belgium and Slovakia. “Professor” (ex-energy minister Halushchenko), “Karlson” (Mindich), “Che Guevara” (ex-deputy PM Chernyshov, allegedly pocketing $500,000 via his wife).

Transcript errors? Irrelevant when shaking down nuclear contractors. Some recordings discuss delaying energy site fortifications for better margins. Why protect infrastructure from missiles when there’s money to extract?

Z, elected to end corruption, watches agencies he tried gutting last summer expose his circle. Two ministers fired. Sanctions called for against Mindich—already abroad. UAH 95 million bail for money launderers still in custody.

Wednesday’s Yermak-EU Ambassador Maternova meeting: damage control theater. She “noted” Z’s statements. Brussels calculates: keep funding a government whose insiders grifted millions from the nuclear sector?

Ukrainian adviser’s summary: “This is ammunition for those... to say, ‘Why are we doing this?’”

The trolley jumped the rails years ago. Z’s own people saying it means we’re screwed.

## Day 1360

*Date: 2025-11-14 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*Missile magnet*

This is so far beyond horror.

[Image]

The pre-dawn shriek of missiles overhead—our weekly Friday alarm clock, right on schedule[1](#footnote-1). You’re out on the balcony overlooking Chorna Hora, high enough that jumping is definitely not an option, watching the sky light up like the world’s worst fireworks show. The missiles scream past, all urgency and drama. Then come the Shahed UAVs with their distinctive lawnmower-from-hell whine, cheaper and slower but just as committed. We’ve learned to tell them apart now, which is either a useful life skill or the most depressing party trick imaginable. Another night in central Kyiv, the middle of Europe, watching Russian, Iranian and North Korean projectiles. You take a deep breath and hope Rheinmetal’s [Skynex](https://www.rheinmetall.com/en/products/air-defence/air-defence-systems/networked-air-defence-skynex) doesn’t run out of ammo.

[Image]

Kyiv has thousands of faces. And some of them we don’t even suspect exist. Behind the facades of 1,000-year old landmarks, the sound of roosters—yes, roosters—and the usual must-sees, there are hidden places you won’t find in guidebooks or read about in [The Guardian](https://www.theguardian.com/europe) or [Kyiv Independent](https://kyivindependent.com/).

Chorna Hora (Black Mountain) is the very center of the mystical-hilly Pechersk Triangle, which includes Lysa and Busova mountains.

The secrecy of this historic area is fully explained by its name. Lysa Hora (Bald Mountain) is shrouded in legends of people being bricked up alive, which were supported by the preserved pagan shrine with a statue of Perun, that is, until the Wickians burned it down in 2013. Busova Hora (Bus’s Mountain) is in the Hryshko Botanical Garden[2](#footnote-2). No one likes to talk about about Chorna Hora because its history is said to be much scarier. Anyway, that’s where I live, right on the blue x.

---

Yesterday evening I regaled the gentlemen at [Tootsie Club](https://uk.tootsie.kiev.ua/) with tales of 1,000 hours of surreptitiously recorded conversations documenting Z’s back office kickback emporium in the energy and defense sectors. Ukraine’s [National Anti-corruption Bureau](https://nabu.gov.ua/en/) (NABU) and Specialized Prosecutor’s Office began their Monday morning drip-feed of audio snippets, implicating ministers and deputy prime ministers in the sort of illicit activities that would make a Chicago alderman blush.

As search and detain warrants pinged into the computer system over the weekend, our two stars—Tymur Mindich and Oleksandr Tsukerman—received what one can only assume was a very courteous heads-up and promptly absconded. Tymur, being Z’s old Kvartal-95 comedy studio partner and bosom buddy, was yesterday subjected to the crushing penalty of a three-year sanction decree. I’m sure he’s weeping into his offshore accounts as we speak.

Sensing the walls closing in, Presidential Office Chief Andriy Yermak unveiled a Friday afternoon masterpiece: [a cock-and-bull testimonial](https://t.me/ermaka2022/7145) about a gang of scoundrels *impersonating* his brother Denys, roaming Kyiv offering plum government posts in exchange for bribes.

[Video: https://youtu.be/PUQL1OBFOE4]

Hours prior, the National Police helpfully arrested said “gang members” in what can only be described as dinner theater without the dinner. The production values of the skit above are undergraduate at best.

Meanwhile, former Defense Minister Rustem Umerov—currently cosplaying as National Security and Defense Council Secretary—discovered urgent, previously unknown business in Turkey and the Middle East. How fortuitous.

Nothing says “I’m definitely not fleeing potential arrest” quite like an impromptu Middle Eastern diplomatic safari.

> I have just arrived in Istanbul. Over the next few days, I’m working in Türkiye and the Middle East to unblock the exchange process. There was an agreement — and it must be implemented. The President of Ukraine set a clear task: Ukrainians must return home from captivity. In the coming days, there will be meetings, as well as in Türkiye. The focus is precisely on this — to resume the exchanges. — Rustem Umerov ([X,](https://x.com/rustem_umerov) November 11, 2025)

Prime Minister Yuliia Svyrydenko contributed her own bit of performance art yesterday: a video solemnly pledging to audit all state-owned enterprises—hundreds of them, mind you—for improprieties.

Roman Chervinsky’s observation that Kremlin rats have taken up residence on Bankova seems, shall we say, understated.

[Video: https://youtu.be/LV-fdQKi48E]

**Hero of the Week** goes to [Ruslan Mahamedrasulov](https://www.gmfus.org/news/ukraines-corruption-scandal-part-two-how-Z-can-get-ahead-it) (Русан Магамедрасулов), a senior NABU detective who was instrumental in the 15-month sting operation, dubbed “Midas.” We hope he is released immediately.

**Photo Essay of the Week** goes to Tyler Hicks of *The New York Times*, for [documenting](https://www.nytimes.com/2025/11/13/world/europe/ukraine-russia-pokrovsk.html) the agonizing fall of Pokrovsk—where I once purchased an enormous watermelon in 2002, in happier times.

Bus/Boz (Latin *Boz nomine*) appears in the *Getica* by Jordanes, written in the 6th century. He is called rex Antorum — “king of the Antes,” a proto-Slavic tribal group. According to Jordanes, after some battles, the Ostrogothic leader *Vinitharius* crucified Boz, eight of his sons, and 70 nobles — apparently to terrify the Antes.

## Day 1363

*Date: 2025-11-17 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*Questions not asked *

My aging, perforated brain this weekend re-wrapped itself around (came to accept, perhaps) the fairly rudimentary and time-proven concept that *a variable created inside a function won’t necessarily be available the next time the function is called*.

[Image]

About two-thirds of the way through [this sanctimonious drivel](https://www.theguardian.com/world/2025/nov/16/Z-pledges-to-clean-up-ukraine-energy-sector-amid-corruption-scandal)—in which Harding and Rankin solemnly intone that Z is “under pressure to prove to European allies that he is serious about tackling corruption”—I recalled Harding’s [slavish interview](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ZyHvrjHnVIg) with Z mere days before this $100m scandal exploded. That puff piece contained precisely zero questions about the industrial-scale graft that was apparently an open secret to everyone except Luke, intrepid Guardian correspondent[1](#footnote-1).

The whiplash is almost comical. Last week: heroic wartime leader, defender of democracy, no follow-ups necessary. This week: “worst scandal of his presidency,” though do note the impressive verbal gymnastics required to avoid saying anything that might puncture the narrative. Even now, even here, we get the usual therapeutic framing—it’s not about corruption, you see, it’s about Ukraine’s “struggle and fight against corruption.”

How inspiring. How completely useless.

One has to admire the sheer nerve required to practice journalism this way—to conduct [a major interview](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ZyHvrjHnVIg) while studiously averting one’s eyes from the story, then to cover that same story later while exhibiting zero self-awareness about one’s own credulity. It’s performance art, really. Bad performance art, but still.

And speaking of performance art: a day later this [masterpiece of evasion](https://abcnews.go.com/Business/wireStory/mike-pompeo-adviser-scrutinized-ukraine-defense-company-boost-127579709) appeared in [The Guardian](http://theguardian.co.uk), the [AP](http://ap.com) reported that Mike Pompeo has joined the advisory board of [Fire Point](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fire_Point_(Ukrainian_firm)), Ukraine’s premier defense contractor—currently under investigation for corruption and facing uncomfortable questions about its ties to Tymur Mindich, who happens to be Z’s old business partner and the star of this particular scandal. You’d think the spectacle of a former U.S. Secretary of State joining the advisory board of a company entangled in the very corruption investigation our Guardian heroes are so delicately chronicling might merit a mention, or at least a moment’s pause. But why let an inconvenient detail like that interrupt the elegantly hedged narrative?

There’s a war on, after all.

Which brings us to the silver lining in all this: if you’re an actual journalist in Ukraine—the kind who asks difficult questions—you don’t need to worry about having an Achilles’ heel. You’re not some tragic figure with a fatal flaw. You’re just an obstacle on the tracks, and eventually you’re going to get flattened. The only real choice is whether you try to derail the oncoming train or step aside and let the Lukes of the world tell everyone how smoothly the trains are running.

[1](#footnote-anchor-1)

[Z pledges to clean up Ukraine’s energy sector amid corruption scandal](https://www.theguardian.com/world/2025/nov/16/Z-pledges-to-clean-up-ukraine-energy-sector-amid-corruption-scandal). *President to overhaul state energy firms after $100m kickback scheme alleged by anti-corruption investigators* ([The Guardian](http://theguardian.co.uk), November 17, 2025)

## Day 1364

*Date: 2025-11-18 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*Variables, scope, persistence*

[Image]

I should write something on this site every once in awhile.

Okay, what’s been going on. Yesterday’s observation about variable scope and persistence has **critical implications** for interactivity in general.

*A variable created inside a function won’t necessarily be available the next time the function is called*. - *[Day 1363 (November 17, 2025)](https://abdymok.substack.com/p/day-1363)*

In a minimalist fullpage, you’ll likely need the thing that works with all frameworks for features like navigation, lazy loading, or tracking which image is currently displayed. The observation warns that **local variables reset between function calls**—meaning if you create a variable inside your `nextImage()` function to track the current slide index, it won’t remember its value next time.

We have fixed that problem[1](#footnote-1).

[Image]

As of this writing, Ukraine’s executive and legislative branch top dogs are all implicated in acts of hideous wrongdoing involving kickbacks and back offices and state-owned enterprises.

Wonderful.

[Video: https://youtu.be/Jk\_nqJRau-g]

In other news, I wrote business plans in two languages, talked to more bank managers than reasonably possible, tried and failed to buy the last remaining affordable [dream house](https://www.olx.ua/d/uk/obyavlenie/prodam-budinok-na-berez-malovnicho-rchki-ros-IDYoW4j.html?isPreviewActive=1&sliderIndex=1) on the Ros’ River, got very angry, was reminded fully and sharply that I’m not a graphic designer, got sternly talked-to by the far-more-patient and impossibly lovely KonMarie during some of the more flaily moments and found (we think) a pretty good alternative for the landing page of this Substack.

Hell of a ride, this life.

[Image]

Russia is still trying to encircle Ukrainian troops around Pokrovsk by attacking from both sides, but keep shooting themselves in the foot by chasing too many objectives at once.

The 51st Army on the east is simultaneously trying to advance north, close the trap, *and* fight around Dobropillia—because why focus on one thing when you can half-ass three? Their Dobropillia push was partly a PR stunt for the August 2025 Alaska summit, but created a vulnerable salient that Ukrainians are now gleefully counterattacking, forcing Russians to defend their rear while trying to advance.

The 2nd Army on the west is equally scattered, trying to close the pocket, clear Pokrovsk itself, and push north—all with degraded, battered units that have taken massive casualties.

Despite infiltrating Pokrovsk in late October and making decent progress, they still can’t finish the job. Ukrainian holdouts in northern Pokrovsk are forcing small Russian groups into miserable street fights.

Now Russians may resort to driving troops in under fog cover since their vaunted small-group infiltration tactics aren’t generating enough mass to actually *clear* the city. It’s a big bloody muddy mess.

[1](#footnote-anchor-1)

This dual-licensing model is intended to keep options open for everybody who wishes to use the fix, from an individual to a multinational corporation.

Two scenarios:

1. **You are an Individual or Community**

You are free to use html free of charge however you see fit: to stalk a celebrity, write about the strengths of that cheese sandwich, distribute mp3s of a friend’s band, make tall dollars from Google AdWords or Amazon. Do as you like.

Provided you or your community are not incorporated under the laws of your country of residence, you are under no obligation – but are encouraged – to contribute alterations and improvements in the form of patches.

2. **You are a Corporation**

You have non-exclusive, royalty-free rights to use the code any way you like, but you are obligated to release any alterations or customisations you make to it, in the form of patches, free of charge, under an open source license. You also grant a non-exclusive, royalty free right to the original developer to distribute your modifications in future versions of the software.

If, however, you wish to create proprietary applications, free of any quid pro quo obligation to release any alterations or customisations to the source code, then you contact me directly.

I liked [this statement,](https://www.facebook.com/nikita.poturaev/posts/pfbid0rnxyXYZaDUcj2AjrWQivqww4a2kTtFJ8iQZkM4HahyWojBQM3sZ1q2dxaCSMrQPNl) dated yesterday, circulated on Facebook by MP Mykyta (Servant of the People faction):

> Statement by representatives of the faction of the Servant of the People political party
>
> in connection with the anti-corruption investigation of the National Anti-corruption Bureau of Ukraine (NABU) and Specialized Anti-corruption Prosecutor’s Office (SAPO).
>
> Aware of the significant increase in the threat to the existence of the Ukrainian state as a result of the exposure of large-scale corruption transactions at the highest levels of state administration and the subsequent crisis of trust in key institutions of power, we, the people’s deputies of Ukraine - representatives of the faction of the political party Servant of the People, declare our political position:
>
> 1. There should be no “untouchables.”
>
> All, without exception, those involved in the anti-corruption investigation of the NABU and SAPO into the activities of the criminal group led by Tymur Mindich must be released immediately. If further investigation reveals new persons with power involved in the activities of this group, the strictest sanctions provided for by the legislation of Ukraine must also be applied to them.
>
> 2. Full support for the NABU and SAPO.
>
> All state authorities, without exception, must assist the NABU and the SAPO in completing the investigation and submitting the materials to the court.
>
> Any attempts to put pressure on anti-corruption bodies, discredit their work, or artificially limit their powers are unacceptable and contradict the national interests of Ukraine.
>
> 3. Coalition of National Resilience.
>
> In order to restore the trust of Ukrainian citizens and our international partners in the state, we call for the immediate start of negotiations between all pro-Ukrainian factions and groups in the Verkhovna Rada of Ukraine on the creation of a coalition of national resilience.
>
> We call on all parliamentary pro-Ukrainian forces to abandon narrow-party and personal priorities that may hinder such a union.
>
> 4. Government of National Resilience – without party quotas and backroom agreements.
>
> After the formation of the coalition of national resilience, it is necessary to begin negotiations between all factions and groups that will be part of it on the formation of the Government of National Resilience.
>
> Such a Government should be:
>
> • formed without party quotas, backroom agreements and “own people”,
>
> • composed of specialists with an impeccable reputation and proven management experience in relevant areas,
>
> • focused on fulfilling a clearly defined anti-crisis mandate: ensuring defense capability, economic stability, energy security and an uncompromising fight against high-ranking corruption.
>
> Members of such a Government should defend national, not narrow political or party interests, and not use their positions as a springboard for future political campaigns.
>
> 5. Constitutional balance of power and strengthening trust in the institution of the President.
>
> The power triangle: the President of Ukraine — the Verkhovna Rada of Ukraine — the Cabinet of Ministers of Ukraine — should return to the framework of the Constitution of Ukraine and be built on the principles of a parliamentary-presidential republic.
>
> It is important that the activities of the Office of the President of Ukraine be as transparent as possible, institutionally regulated and free from any informal influence of persons involved in corrupt practices.
>
> We are convinced that the President of Ukraine will use the results of this investigation and the clear request of society as an opportunity to cleanse and renew his inner circle, to strengthen trust in the institution of the Head of State both within the country and among our international partners.
>
> We appeal to Ukrainian civil society, Ukrainian media, as well as to our international friends and partners with an appeal to support this initiative as a necessary condition for continuing the joint struggle to preserve the independence and sovereignty of Ukraine.
>
> In discord - defeat.
>
> In unity, cleansing and justice - victory.
>
> Glory to Ukraine!

I support any initiative abide by Ukraine’s Constitution, although I am uncomfortable associating myself with Mykyta in any other way, shape or form.

Timothy wrote a long post about negotiating with Russia.

[[Image]Thinking about...

How to Negotiate with Russia

Last night, Russia attacked Ukrainian civilians with more than five hundred drones, cruise missiles, and rockets. Most were shot down, but one rocket got through to Ternopil in western Ukraine, struck an apartment building, and killed at least twenty-five civilians, including three children. Across the country apartment buildings, shops, post offices…

Read more

7 months ago · 882 likes · 47 comments · Timothy Snyder](https://snyder.substack.com/p/how-to-negotiate-with-russia?utm_source=substack&utm_campaign=post_embed&utm_medium=web)

Maybe western intellectuals, media figures, and international admirers might consider a discreet intervention—perhaps confronting Z directly, preferably in person over a couple of papa cocktails, to explain in a friendly and admiring way why golden-toilet, mafia-style governance may be entertaining spectacle, but hardly provides a sound foundation for deeper partnership.

One reason we liked Mykyta’s appeal is because it calls for abiding by Ukraine’s Constitution.

---

**Five Blind Mice and the Peace Plan Elephant**

Five Financial Times journalists—*five*—teamed up to produce 746 words on Russia and America’s “sweeping new proposal” to end the war in Ukraine[1](#footnote-1). Christopher Miller, Anastasia Stognei, Amy Mackinnon, Phil Georgiadis, and Jamie Smyth pooled their considerable talents to explain how Kirill Dmitriev, Putin’s sovereign wealth fund guy, helped draft a 28-point framework that would have Ukraine cede the Donbas, slash its military in half, ban foreign troops, abandon long-range weapons, recognize Russian as an official language, and presumably install a golden toilet in the Kremlin as a thank-you gift.

What these intrepid correspondents somehow failed to mention—perhaps it slipped through the cracks between paragraphs about oil price fluctuations and framework stages—is that while Steve Witkoff was hand-delivering this magnificent document, Russian cruise missiles were simultaneously pulverizing apartment blocks in Ternopil, killing dozens of civilians.

But why burden readers with such tedious details? After all, the FT team was clearly too busy explaining why this plan is “very comfortable for Putin” and “heavily tilted towards Russia” to waste column inches on the inconvenient reality that comfort, in Putin’s case, typically involves Russia’s genocidal war in Ukraine.

The article does note that “oil prices fell sharply on reports of the proposal”—because nothing says serious journalism quite like prioritizing Brent crude futures over the bodies being pulled from rubble. Kevin Book from ClearView Energy Partners gets quoted discussing “oversupply concerns.” One imagines the residents of those obliterated apartments would be thrilled to know their deaths coincided with such promising market movements.

Perhaps the FT is pioneering a new genre: peace process reporting where the actual war is treated as atmospheric background noise, like traffic outside a conference room window. The missiles? The civilians? Just speed bumps on the road to that sweet, sweet diplomatic “framework.”

Numerous attempts to contact the five-person FT dream team and ask why mass civilian casualties didn’t merit a single sentence have, so far, been unsuccessful.

## Day 1367 

*Date: 2025-11-21 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*Surrender diplomacy meets Golden Toilet mafia*

We left a blank yesterday for Z to explain how he plans to handle the golden toilet mafia scandal—Operation Midas, featuring over 1,000 wiretaps spanning 15 months, the equivalent of 94 *War and Peace* novels documenting back-office corruption. Instead, he told his parliamentary faction: “Everyone should work for Ukraine, that’s how it will be.” Translation: I intend to continue running this country like Slobodan Milošević.

The atmosphere in Kyiv increasingly resembles Belgrade in late 1998—alternative music, biting satire, political art, social life as coping mechanism, cultural resilience masking exhaustion. A city sensing catastrophe, defiance mixing with dread.

Enter Trump’s “peace plan,” timing so exquisite it might be satire. After decades posturing as America’s toughest dealmaker, he’s revealed his diplomatic genius: capitulation rebranded as statesmanship. The 28-point proposal isn’t negotiation—it’s Putin’s maximalist 2022 Istanbul demands with an American stamp.

> [US] officials said they received “positive feedback” from Ukraine’s Secretary of the National Security and Defense Council Rustem Umerov during discussions with Witkoff in Miami in recent weeks, but stopped short of saying Kyiv signed off on the deal.

[Image]

> “The plan was drawn up immediately following discussions with one of the most senior members of Z’s administration, Umerov,” one of the officials said. “So Umerov agreed to the majority of this plan, and he made several modifications to it, which we included and presented it to Z - [The New York Post](https://nypost.com/2025/11/20/us-news/senior-us-officials-confirm-details-of-28-point-plan-to-end-ukraine-war/) (November 21, 2025)

Umerov is Ukraine’s former minister of defense implicated in shady real estate deals in Boca Raton, et cetera. How those UAH 23 billion ($550 million) were divvied up [last year](https://www.kmu.gov.ua/npas/pytannia-delehuvannia-povnovazhen-dlia-zdiisnennia-oboronnykh-zakupiv-1191r) remains a mystery.

> At the core of the deal lies the partition of Ukraine – to be recognised by everyone except Ukraine itself (they won’t subject it to further humiliation). The partition is harsh, but at the same time modest if you compare it to the original ambitions: Crimea, two practically destroyed regions, plus a small extra strip in the South, all paid for with at least half a million lives and an incalculable amount of money. Not exactly a massive prize, frankly. “The world’s second army” failed, in four years, to achieve what Manstein or Rokossovsky managed in a matter of months (back and forth). All in all, it’s a failure whose consequences will only be fully grasped later, in all their terrible magnitude. But at first, of course, it will be sold as a colossal victory. - [Practicing philosopher Volodymyr Pastukhov](https://t.me/pastukhov_eng/45) (November 19, 2025)

The arithmetic of humiliation is simple: Ukraine surrenders 20% of its territory, slashes its military in half, constitutionally renounces NATO, and trusts “security guarantees” from a president whose promises expire faster than milk. Russia’s concessions? Precisely zero. Putin receives sanctions relief, G8 reinstatement, joint investment vehicles with Washington, and 50% profits from his own frozen assets.

[The Institute for the Study of War](https://understandingwar.org/research/russia-ukraine/russian-offensive-campaign-assessment-november-20-2025/) spells out what Trump cannot grasp: forcing Ukrainian withdrawal from Donetsk creates indefensible lines favoring Russian reattack. The Kremlin explicitly states it will achieve war goals “diplomatically or militarily”—this “peace” merely lets Russia rest, reconstitute, and return against a neutered, territorially amputated Ukraine.

Most obscene is the process: developed with Moscow, imposed on Kyiv without consultation, revealed to allies through leaks. Trump promised to stop Putin’s war in 24 hours. A year later, he’s delivered Ukraine gift-wrapped to subjugation while Z channels Milošević and the golden toilets flush.

[Image]

---

Proposals from [Witkoff-Dmitriev](https://www.axios.com/2025/11/20/trump-ukraine-peace-plan-28-points-russia) vs counter proposals from [Europe](https://www.reuters.com/business/finance/full-text-european-counter-proposal-us-ukraine-peace-plan-2025-11-23/):

**Point 10a** - US to receive compensation for the guarantee (new detail)

**Point 10d** - New clause: “If Ukraine launches a missile at Moscow or St. Petersburg without cause, the security guarantee will be deemed invalid” (this appears only in the 28-point plan, actually)

**Point 14** - Completely restructured frozen assets provisions:

- European proposal: Ukraine compensated through frozen Russian assets that remain frozen until Russia pays
- 28-point plan: Specific $100B splits with US getting 50% profits, plus joint US-Russia investment vehicle

## Major Deletions/Changes in European Proposal:

**Point 3** - Explicitly noted as DELETED (the Reuters draft stated expectations that Russia won’t invade neighbors and NATO won’t expand)

**Point 6** - Military size cap changed from **600,000** to **800,000** personnel

**Point 7** - Significantly softened:

- 28-point: Ukraine constitutionally banned from NATO, NATO statutorily prevented from admitting Ukraine
- European: “Ukraine joining NATO depends on consensus of NATO members, which does not exist” (much weaker language)

**Point 8** - Weakened from absolute ban to conditional:

- 28-point: “NATO agrees not to station troops in Ukraine”
- European: “NATO agrees not to permanently station troops **under its command** in Ukraine **in peacetime**“ (allows temporary/wartime deployments)

**Point 9** - Changed from “European fighter jets” to “NATO fighter jets”

**Point 10** - Missing the Moscow/St. Petersburg missile clause from 28-point plan

**Point 15** - Restructured:

- 28-point: US-Russian bilateral working group
- European: Multilateral taskforce including US, Ukraine, Russia, **and Europeans**

**Point 20** - Dramatically reduced:

- 28-point: Extensive mutual educational programs, media rights, anti-Nazi provisions for both countries
- European: Only requires Ukraine to adopt EU rules on religious tolerance and linguistic minorities (Russia obligations removed)

**Point 21** - Completely rewritten territories section:

- 28-point: Specific recognition of Crimea, Luhansk, Donetsk as Russian; frozen status for Kherson/Zaporizhia; demilitarized buffer zone in Donetsk
- European: Vague “negotiations on territorial swaps will start from Line of Contact” with commitment not to recover territory militarily (no specific territorial concessions listed)

**Point 25** - Timeline change:

- 28-point: Elections in 100 days
- European: Elections “as soon as possible”

**Point 26** - Amnesty clause DELETED entirely from European proposal (28-point included full amnesty for all wartime actions)

**Point 27** - Name change: “Peace Council” → “Board of Peace”

In summary, the European proposal appears significantly **more favorable to Ukraine** than the 28-point plan:

- Larger military allowed (800K vs 600K)
- NATO membership language much softer
- No specific territorial concessions spelled out
- No amnesty for Russian actions
- Frozen assets go to Ukraine, not split with US/Russia joint ventures
- More multilateral oversight (Europeans included)
- Russia gets fewer specific concessions on education/cultural policies

  ---

What does practicing philosopher Volodymyr Pastukhov say?

> Trump’s plan is likely to be stalled, buried, or both – for a while. But this is not what matters. What matters is the idea behind it: wars may end through a harsh compromise rather than through the moral certainty of knocking out the aggressor. The idea is distasteful, but it is historically sound.
>
> Yes, the plan is ugly. Yes, it allows perpetrators to escape accountability, perhaps even to die peacefully in their beds. But world history is stitched from such unlovely bargains. It may be precisely because of them that humanity has managed to survive at all.
>
> The war moves to the stalls. Ukraine has achieved the essential thing: the theatre building still stands. From here on, the victor will be the one who stages the more compelling performance of life – the side that builds a world more stable, livable, and attractive than its rival can. It is time to move on to the next act, rather than mourning the ending we wanted but cannot recover. - [Telegram](https://t.me/pastukhov_eng/49) (November 24, 2025)

Team USA has been notoriously bad at fighting and winning wars during my lifetime. The thought of Dan and JD working out *harsh compromises* we have to make in order to end the largest land war in Europe in more than 80 years is troubling.

Best to avoid unlovely bargains altogether.

## Day 1373 

*Date: 2025-11-27 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*Thanksgiving and Tushino*

[Image]

There’s a particular satisfaction to scraping resin from an old pipe at dawn—the quiet ritual of it, working the paperclip or knife tip into the bowl’s crevices, coaxing out those tarry flecks. When you finally inhale and feel it creep into your skull, there’s a small victory in it. You’ve made something from nothing. The sun’s barely up, the world’s still quiet and you’re wearing that charismatic pair of [DoDo](http://dodosocks.com) socks again.

Happy Thanksgiving!

---

Raise your hand if you were at Tushino Airfield in September 1991.

I drove a borrowed Kopeika—a VAZ-2101, the “one kopeck car”—about thirty minutes north from the Hotel Moskva, where Soviet deputies still kept rooms. The Berezovzhskaya Accords were months away. The Baltics had already bolted. Ukraine too. The USSR was coming apart in real time.

We picked up two girls from Pokrovskaya Gora along the Klyazma and headed for the airfield. What we found there was half a million Russians losing their minds.

[Video: https://youtu.be/\_W7wqQwa-TU]

I was wondering this morning why the Metallica concert came back to me. Then I realized: Thanksgiving 1992. That’s when I moved to Moscow for good. By then, the chaos had settled. By late 1993, the experiment was over.

---

Kurt does an excellent job explaining the current Trump-Ukraine-Russia mess.

[Video: https://youtu.be/cIQfmtR71eM]

But he leaves out whether Ukraine can function for the next year or so as an independent state—at least with the current crop of political leaders, whose ability to absorb foreign assistance depends on ignoring wartime kleptocracy documented in hundreds of hours of leaked recordings. Dreams of peace stay dreams. The best Z and his top managers can hope for is a lull in the fighting. Putin won’t agree to stop killing people until he can claim victory—and he can’t do that now.

I listened to Kim Barker and David Sanger from [The New York Times](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=pHqzPDgXrHo).

[Video: https://youtu.be/pHqzPDgXrHo]

Kim understates the *Coffee, Cocktails & Crime* aspect of the story—the slush fund management style of governance adopted after repelling Russian invaders around Kyiv in early 2022. She [was in Ternopil](https://www.nytimes.com/video/world/europe/100000010538311/the-aftermath-of-a-deadly-strike-in-western-ukraine.html) after Russian missile flattened a residential building last week and can relate better than David, who takes the opportunity to model a suit and read from a teleprompter.

Simon, Z’s hagiographer, writes a zillion words (3,800+) for [The Atlantic](https://www.theatlantic.com/national-security/2025/11/ukraine-peace-push-russia-trump-Z/685045/) without mentioning once that our president and his chief of staff, Andriy Yermak, along with ex Defense Minister turned National Security and Defense Council Secretary Rustem Umerov, have been, er, enriching themselves in back office schemes while their country is existentially threatened[1](#footnote-1).

And this just in from The Economist[2](#footnote-2).

[Image]

Why should anyone agonize over Ukraine’s “moment of maximum diplomatic danger” while studiously ignoring the grift? Z’s government isn’t just unpopular—it’s a kleptocracy on a drip feed of Western billions, documented in hundreds of hours of leaked recordings that apparently no one at The Economist bothered to listen to. You want Europe to shovel another EUR140 billion into this bonfire?

Capital won’t return if Russia might attack again? Try this: capital won’t return because Z, Yermak, and Umerov have been running the country like a mob franchise while lecturing the West about courage under fire. Putin’s paying in blood for Ukrainian soil. They’re paying in cash for golden parachutes. The difference is Putin knows what he’s buying.

“So it goes” appears throughout [Slaughterhouse-Five](https://antilogicalism.com/wp-content/uploads/2018/04/slaughterhouse-five.pdf). It’s Vonnegut’s deadpan shrug at death—a fatalistic hiccup that pops up whenever someone dies. The phrase captures the worldview that fighting fate is pointless, the perfect philosophy for checking out of responsibility entirely, depending on how charitable you’re feeling.

[Image]

Yesterday, Z awarded “Golden Hearts” to Irish medics training Ukrainian doctors to stop frontline bleeding[1](#footnote-1). Noble stuff.

Meanwhile, 3,392 miles west, Trump, [fighting sleep](https://www.nytimes.com/2025/12/02/us/politics/trump-sleep-cabinet-meeting.html), called Ukraine “chaos” at a cabinet meeting, claiming he’s “settled eight wars” and his people are “over in Russia right now” working on number nine. “Not easy. What a mess.”

Related cases. Foreign press stays quiet, though. Acknowledging them means admitting an inconvenient truth about their previous reporting and, er, Z.

---

**Speaking of inconvenient truths and the instinct to write one’s way out of them:** Olivia Nuzzi thought she could pull it off. The political reporter’s new book, *American Canto*, was supposed to reframe her scandal—the revelation that she’d been in a months-long relationship with Robert F. Kennedy Jr., whom she’d profiled, leading to her departure from New York magazine after violating conflict-of-interest standards. An unnamed editor had told her, “You could write your way out of it.”

Instead, the book’s debut became its own fresh disaster. Days before publication, her ex-fiancé Ryan Lizza alleged on Substack that she’d also slept with another politician she profiled—former South Carolina governor Mark Sanford—and that her Kennedy liaison went beyond sexts to include acting as an unpaid political operative, feeding him confidential source material. Vanity Fair, which had just hired her as West Coast editor, launched a review. A spokesperson told the Times they were “taken by surprise” and “looking at all the facts.”

[The Colombia Journalism Review](https://www.cjr.org/news/olivia-nuzzi-american-canto.php) notes the bitter irony: Nuzzi once shared a piece criticizing the Hollywood stereotype of female reporters sleeping with sources, asking on X, “Why does Hollywood think female reporters sleep with their sources?” Turns out, sometimes stereotypes write themselves. And sometimes, writing one’s way out proves trickier than it seems.

So it goes.

## Day 1381

*Date: 2025-12-05 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*Putin wants all of Donbas*

[Image]

Vladimir Putin just blurted — from India, of all places — that Russia wants all of Donbas … and basically as much of Ukraine as possible. That means eastern and southern Ukraine — Kherson, Zaporizhia, Mykolaiv, Odesa, Dnipropetrovsk, Kharkiv. If it’s north of the Black Sea and east of the Dnipro — you’re on the map. Echoes of “peace talks”? Hard pass. 🛑

Meanwhile, a [leaked call](https://www.politico.eu/article/european-leaders-warn-us-could-betray-ukraine-in-leaked-call/) among top European leaders (Friedrich Merz, Emmanuel Macron and co.) reportedly warned that the U.S. negotiation track might stab Ukraine in the back. Quote: “They are playing games with both you and us.” And Finnish and NATO voices chimed in: “We must not leave Ukraine and Volodymyr alone with these guys.”

Then there’s the newly released [2025 U.S. National Security Strategy](https://www.whitehouse.gov/wp-content/uploads/2025/12/2025-National-Security-Strategy.pdf) — tea spilled, everyone. Big red flag for Europe: according to the document, Washington now wants the continent to “take more responsibility for its own defense” while the U.S. pivots to Portland. It paints Europe as losing its civilizational backbone, overregulated, demographically and economically shaky — and in need of someone to shake it awake.

**But here’s the twist Europe won’t say out loud:** while publicly wringing hands about American abandonment, they’ve been privately tolerating Ukrainian corruption for years. [The New York Times just dropped](https://www.nytimes.com/2025/01/31/world/europe/ukraine-Z-corruption.html) a forensic autopsy showing how Z’s government systematically dismantled the oversight mechanisms Western donors demanded — stacking supervisory boards with loyalists, leaving seats strategically empty, rewriting company charters to keep outside experts from “poking around.”

[Image]

The money shot? [$100 million kickback scheme](https://www.nytimes.com/2025/01/31/world/europe/ukraine-Z-corruption.html) at Ukraine’s nuclear power company. Z’s inner circle accused of laundering it. Contractors paying 15 percent grift tax. His government blamed the supervisory board for not stopping it — *after* neutering that same board.

Norway’s special envoy to Ukraine, unfiltered: “We do care about good governance, but we have to accept that risk. Because it’s war.”

**Translation: They knew. They shrugged. The money kept flowing.**

The play-by-play:

- Europe told to reinvent its own security from scratch.
- NATO’s safety net fraying in real-time.
- Ukraine dangling while U.S.–Russia talks sail ahead.
- **Putin broadcasts ambition. Z’s administration systematically blocks anti-corruption safeguards. Washington disengages. Everyone pretends this is fine.**
- No cleanup = no postwar reconstruction billions. EU and NATO membership? Also toast.

The supervisory board saga is the war in miniature: Z ran in 2019 to end corruption. Post-invasion, he relaxed anti-corruption rules, rehabilitated figures he’d once called criminals, gutted the oversight his Western backers thought they’d purchased.

When boards pushed back—foreign experts at Ukrenergo resigned after a politically motivated CEO firing—Europe froze some payments, then kept the taps open. Abandoning Ukraine looked bad for the photo ops.

Grotesque irony: the same European leaders warning Trump might sell Ukraine out spent years creating “a permissive environment for backsliding.” They commissioned reports. Got warnings. Filed them away.

The tell: Z’s government rewrote company charters *as Trump returned*—perfect timing, guardrails off.

Meanwhile, the press churns. Nuzzi and Mendel: polished branches of the same tree. Confidence over clarity, narrative over nuance, performance over journalism. Immaculate finishes, substance negotiable.

Europe postures about American betrayal while bankrolling known corruption. Z dismantles oversight, blames the boards he destroyed. The press glamorizes personalities.

Putin broadcasts territorial ambitions. Everyone else performs their script while the house burns.

## Day 1384 

*Date: 2025-12-08 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*Europe's civilizational erasure*

[Image]

---

[Image]

Sigh. The Kremlin has said it expects Kushner to be doing the main work on [drafting](https://www.reuters.com/world/kremlin-adviser-says-putin-us-envoy-witkoff-understand-each-other-2025-12-05/) a possible deal. And jackass Donald Jr. is spouting, again.

[Video: https://youtu.be/niarA8cZ7L4]

As for the the [2025 NSS](https://www.whitehouse.gov/wp-content/uploads/2025/12/2025-National-Security-Strategy.pdf), its Europe section opens with concerns about European military spending, then immediately pivots to the real emergency: not enough babies and too many regulations. Europe faces “civilizational erasure” and will be “unrecognizable in 20 years,” the document warns, in prose that reads like a Substack panic attack achieved sentience and infiltrated the State Department.

The contradiction at the document’s core is exquisite. America champions “genuine democracy” while celebrating that “patriotic European parties” are gaining influence—parties that, inconveniently, keep losing elections. European governments “trample on basic principles of democracy to suppress opposition,” we’re told, but the solution is apparently to encourage the opposition doing the suppressing. When your democratic vision requires overriding election results because voters chose wrong, perhaps reconsider the “genuine” part.

The Ukraine bits deserve special recognition for their euphemistic brilliance. We need an “**expeditious cessation of hostilities**” to “**stabilize European economies**” and “**reestablish strategic stability with Russia**.” Translation: Let Putin keep his conquered territory so Germany can buy cheap gas again. The document actually complains that Europe has “unrealistic expectations for the war”—apparently expecting to not reward aggression with territorial concessions is now unrealistic. Bold stance from a strategy document theoretically opposed to “adversarial domination.”

Most remarkable is the fretting about European “external dependencies” while advocating policies that would make Ukraine permanently dependent on Russian imperial benevolence. German chemical companies building plants in China using Russian gas is presented as a crisis, but the proposed solution is... giving Russia what it wants in Ukraine? Twisted.

The “civilizational self-confidence” rhetoric would be touching if it weren’t so transparently about demographics and cultural anxiety dressed up as strategic analysis. Europe needs to “remain European,” whatever that means, while simultaneously opening markets to American goods and doing exactly what Washington demands. Sovereignty for thee, subordination for me.

The document’s vision of “aligned sovereign nations” operating independently but in concert sounds inspiring until you realize it’s describing the exact function of institutions like the EU and NATO—the very “transnational bodies” the text excoriates. The alternative framework appears to be vibes-based coordination where European countries spontaneously agree with American preferences, which is either magical thinking or imperialism with better marketing.

Europe doesn’t need American guidance to rediscover its “former greatness.”

The subtext isn’t even subtle: Europe’s problems aren’t military or economic, they’re existential and civilizational. And America’s role is to save Europe from itself by encouraging the political forces that would dismantle the postwar liberal order.

It’s a strategy built on nostalgia for a past that never existed, contempt for institutions that actually work, and a willingness to sacrifice Ukrainian sovereignty for cheap energy. Call it what it is: managed decline with a civilizational aesthetic.

## Day 1385

*Date: 2025-12-09 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*The open society's unfinished business*

[Image]

The weather has been very warm.

[Image]

Are Robert and [Trey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trey_Yingst) related?

---

[Image]

---

The fall of the Berlin Wall validated the open society model for, er, at least ten years, maybe 15, depending on how you count. The Revolution of Dignity came after a years-long malaise following the Orange Revolution. It marked the end of the democratic recession.

The West still retains considerable leverage over Russian weakness, economic and military. Victory for Moscow is far from inevitable. But we might first answer a more fundamental question: Why does one combatant investigate its own corruption during existential crisis while the other simply murders investigators and calls the result statecraft?

The answer, of course, is that corruption *costs wars*. It siphons funds from ammunition to oligarchs’ pockets, places incompetents in command positions through bribery rather than merit, and corrodes the citizen’s willingness to sacrifice when they know officials are enriching themselves. Ukraine investigates corruption precisely *because* it’s fighting for survival—every hryvnia stolen from defense procurement is a Ukrainian life endangered. Russia’s model—Putin’s palace, his cronies’ yachts, the systematic looting of state resources—is not a sign of strength but of a military that can afford to waste men like ammunition because it views both as equally expendable.

The tragedy is that Ukraine’s necessary anti-corruption efforts weaken it at the precise moment the country needs it most. Russia suffers no such impediment because it has simply abandoned the pretense that the state serves anyone beyond the kleptocrats who own it. One might call this an advantage, until one recalls that this very corruption is why Russian logistics collapse, why generals send untrained conscripts into minefields, why Moscow must recruit North Korean infantry to sustain offensives.

Back to first principles, then. And not a moment too soon.

## Day 1387

*Date: 2025-12-11 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*The window has closed*

[Image]

The window for any non-catastrophic outcome has slammed shut. Team USA has given up leverage to Russia and is abandoning a partner struggling against an authoritarian regime’s genocidal war of conquest.

Trump says we have a ***massive corruption situation**:*

**I still haven’t read the 20-point peace plan worked out by Ukraine, Europe and Team USA**. It’s secret. There are rumors part of it provides for chopping contested areas along with 1,200-kilometer front-line into zones: a demilitarized zone, an occupied zone devoid of heavy weapons, et cetera. This won’t work for the same reasons the OSCE SMM failed a decade ago. Russia will continue invading until someone punches Putin in the face.

[According to ZN](https://zn.ua/ukr/WORLD/novij-mirnij-plan-trampa-do-choho-primushujut-ukrajinu-analiz-chotirokh-dokumentiv.html), Trump is looking at four documents:
